and 4 and implement those relaxed interpreters.

Stepping down from the design of systems to the local code design, we can
also mitigate the problem of bare string keys. Constants will help, yet won’t
prevent occasional copy-paste errors:
leftControllerName :: String
leftControllerName = "left b ctrl"

invalid4 :: Hdl
invalid4 =
  [ SetupController "left booster" leftControllerName aaaController86Passpor

    -- invalid controller specified:
  , RegisterComponent "right b ctrl" "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
  ]

Additionally, we could utilize newtypes for a safer code:
newtype ControllerName = ControllerName String
newtype ComponentIndex = ComponentIndex String

New types prevent specifying wrong arguments by making them explicitly
typed. ControllerName is no longer a bare string, and it’s not the same as
ComponentIndex: neither for the developer nor the compiler. Many errors can
suddenly go away if you wrap all your bare types into new types. I’m
avoiding this in my examples for clarity, but I would use new types more
often in production.

It’s still possible to mess up though. You’ll easily find more other small
tricks and tips to improve the situation, and there must be a book similar to
Code Complete by Steve McConnell, just for functional languages. Such
practices will definitely improve your DSLs. I would write such a book, and
I’m sure it will be the next big bestseller because functional programming is
in big demand today.

Yet, we’re interested in a more rigorous design. What can we do with the
language? How to make invalid scripts irrepresentable? The pattern I’ll show
below will help with this. It’s very common in functional programming
because it’s based on continuations-- a way to compose computations into a
single one-directed chain. Interpretable languages with continuations all
together enable a more functional code. You’ll see that this pattern is deeply
rooted in the Free monads we’ll be talking about very soon.

The Hdl language should be updated. We need to make the two methods
chained somehow--so that a developer couldn’t ignore SetupController
before calling RegisterComponent. You’ll see how continuation for
SetupController does the job in listing 5.2. I’ll add more methods into the
Hdl language for better presentability, although those methods don’t fall
under its “hardware definition” nature. The SRP says these methods
shouldn’t be here, and the ISP recommends putting them in their interfaces.
We’ll do that later.

Listing 5.2 Hdl language with the continuation pattern

newtype Controller = Controller ControllerName
data Status = StatusOk | StatusFail String
type Message = String

type Hdl = [HdlMethod]

data HdlMethod
  = SetupController
       DeviceName                    #A
       ControllerName                #A
       ComponentPassport             #A
       (Controller -> Hdl)           #B
  | RegisterComponent Controller ComponentIndex ComponentPassport
  | ReadSensor Controller ComponentIndex (Either String Measurement -> Hdl)
  | GetStatus Controller (Either String Status -> Hdl)
  | Report Message


All the methods are self-descriptive except maybe Report. It’s not clear
where the message goes. The Hdl language doesn’t define that. The
interpreter of this method will decide what to do: to print the message to the
console, store it into a database, or push it to some external service. Let’s
agree that this is not important for now.

What really needs some explanation is the continuation fields. They all have
types of functions with the return type Hdl. Three methods have continuation
fields, so only them can be (must be) continued by some internal Hdl script.
And this script must accept the argument of a type the continuation specifies.
It’s kind of a “return” type of method. The following is a scheme of the
pattern:
MethodName ParamType1 ParamType2 ... (ReturnType -> nextScript)

The last item here is a continuation function that must be run after
interpreting this particular method is finished. It becomes impossible to
ignore this continuation because every other way can only work with this
return type. In our case, all the methods expect an instance of the newly
introduced Controller type, and the only SetupController method encodes
its creation. This forces us to write nested scripts like the following:

Listing 5.3 Tree-like script

script :: Hdl
script =
  [ SetupController "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passport
    ( \lCtrl ->
      [ RegisterComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
      , RegisterComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-p" aaaPressure02Passport
      , SetupController "right booster" "right b ctrl" aaaController86Passpo
        ( \rCtrl ->
          [ RegisterComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
          , RegisterComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-p" aaaPressure02Passport
          , ReadSensor lCtrl "nozzle1-t"
            ( \eMeasurement -> [ Report (show eMeasurement) ] )
          ]
        )
      ]
    )
  ]

You’ve got the idea, right? I’ll understand if you are concerned that this code
is awkward. Readable--kind of; simple--absolutely; ugly--no doubt. No
worries, functional languages like Haskell and Scala offer good tools to
reduce the clumsy boilerplate. And by the way, Lisp developers may find this
code more or less familiar. Indeed, compare it with a Clojure variant:
(def aaaController86Passport "aaaController86Passport")
(def aaaTemperature25Passport "aaaTemperature25Passport")
(def aaaPressure02Passport "aaaPressure02Passport")
(defn setup-controller [device-name controller-name passp next] ())
(defn register-component [controller component-idx passp] ())

(def script [
    (setup-controller "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passport
        (fn [controller] ([
            (register-component controller "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passp
            (register-component controller "nozzle1-p" aaaPressure02Passport
            ]
            )))
    ])

It isn’t complete, isn’t well-composed, maybe not idiomatic enough. Just a
sample of similarities between our languages. Essentially, we’re doing the
same: we’re writing the logic declaratively, and its tree-like structure with
lists is intended to imitate sequential actions. The logic may seem imperative
a little, and this is not a disaster. Every capable paradigm can mimic some
other paradigm. I see this as very encouraging because the most exciting
things are lying on edge in-between.

Modeling with ADTs isn't rocket science, at least for now. We just scratched
the surface. There is much more below, more layers of matter, more things to
learn. How about parametrized ADTs? Generalized ADTs? Existential
ADTs? These concepts deserve a better explanation and showcasing.
Domain-driven design with advanced algebraic data types--sounds like a nice
book to have! I’ll do my best to introduce you to some of these ideas here as
well.

Why not model something more complicated?

5.2.3 Modeling multiple interacting eDSLs
Hdl was a god-like domain-specific language, and script was a verbose
business logic scenario. I would not keep the Hdl language in its current
form. It violates the SRP and does too much. Shall we add more languages
and distribute the responsibilities across them? Yes, of course. We’re
mandated to do so by our design principles. Let’s get started refactoring then-
-and spoiler alert--the continuations technique will bring even more mess
instead of removing it.
Before that, I should clarify why we’re paying this much attention to the
design of such eDSLs. There are two key points.

     You’ll see the same approach in building ASTs (Abstract Syntax Trees),
     including ASTs a typical compiler constructs after parsing a code, and
     those ASTs we’d like to implement for an external domain-specific
     language for the Andromeda control software.
     This is a base for more advanced and more convenient approaches.
     Thus, Free monads are based on continuations, but instead of exhibiting
     this magic to the developer, they wrap it into a very convenient, clean
     form.

Besides that, it seems a good idea to try various design techniques and
discuss their pros and cons.

Now, when this is clear, let’s jump in. Breaking the HdlMethod will give us
three responsibilities:

     defining a device (SetupController and RegisterComponent) - this will
     be the Hdl language;
     interacting with a device (ReadSensor and GetStatus) - a new
     DeviceControl language will do that;
     report something with Report - let this be in LogicControl.

If you try to put them into three languages, you’ll immediately face a big
problem: the languages can’t be independent, and they have to reference each
other. From the one hand, initializing a device should return the
corresponding instance of the controller:
init device -> get a controller                  -- HDL script

From the other hand, this instance should be utilized in LogicControl:
use controller -> query the device               -- LogicControl script

And from the third side, there is no any real returning mechanism in our
languages, and we can’t run HDL from LogicControl expecting the result:
ctrl <- runHdl:               -- LogicControl script
  ctrl <- init device               -- HDL script
  return ctrl                       -- ! not possible currently
use ctrl                            -- back to LogicControl script

The only surrogate we have for “returning” values is putting those values into
the nested scripts, with continuations. So for now we have to put
LogicControl into HDL even if this doesn’t seem correct:
runHdl:                                  -- LogicControl script
  init device                            -- HDL script
    (\ctrl -> use ctrl)                  -- nested LogicControl script
do something else                        -- back to LogicControl script

You’ll can see this from the next listing and figure, which eventually have the
same number 5.4 will explain this. The continuation fields cause this
entanglement, when each of the two HdlMethod and DeviceControlMethod
nest a continuation with LogicControl:

Listing 5.4 Highly entangled languages

type Hdl = [HdlMethod]

data HdlMethod
  = SetupController DeviceName ControllerName ComponentPassport (Controller
  | RegisterComponent Controller ComponentIndex ComponentPassport


type DeviceControl = [DeviceControlMethod]

data DeviceControlMethod
  = GetStatus Controller (Either String Status -> LogicControl)
  | ReadSensor Controller ComponentIndex (Either String Measurement -> Logic


type LogicControl = [LogicControlMethod]

data LogicControlMethod
  = EvalHdl Hdl                                       #C
  | EvalDeviceControl DeviceControl                   #C
  | Report Message


Figure 5.4 Highly entangled languages
The SetupController method produces a Controller that we pass into the
internal LogicControl script. We want to call RegisterComponent with it, -
so we do a recursive nesting of another Hdl script with EvalHdl. When we
finished preparing a device, ReadSensor may be invoked, - so we need to
pass through LogicControl again and jump into DeviceControl using
EvalDeviceControl. And so on, and so forth. Listing 5.5 shows this scenario
in a nutshell:

Listing 5.5 Not a plain LogicControl script

script :: LogicControl
script =
  [ EvalHdl                                                        #A
    [ SetupController "device" "ctrl" passp1 (\ctrl ->             #B
      [ EvalHdl                                                    #C
        [ RegisterComponent ctrl "therm" passp2 ]                  #D
      , EvalDeviceControl (readAndReport ctrl)                     #E
      ]
    )]
  ]
  where
    readAndReport :: Controller -> DeviceControl
    readAndReport ctrl =
      [ ReadSensor ctrl "therm" (\eMeasurement ->
        [ Report (show eMeasurement) ])
      ]
Notice that it makes sense to break the script apart and assign its blocks to
meaningful functions like readAndReport. Not that much though.

This approach works well for compiler-related tasks; in particular, we’ll
return to recursive algebraic data types when it comes to creating an external
DSL. A quick sidestep to the code from the parser proofs this:
data Expr = ConstructorExpr Constructor                    #A

data Constructor = Constructor String ArgDef               #B

data ArgDef
  = NoneArgs
  | Args [Expr]                                            #C


However, this design becomes problematic in our task with the internal
domain-oriented and imperative language. The coupling between the
languages is catastrophic. The code in listing 5.4 violates so many design
principles, including low coupling, high cohesion, that its presence in the
code becomes a problem rather than a solution. It’s the strongest design
smell, an obvious design mistake.

Also, trying to put those three languages into three modules will lead to
cyclic dependencies between modules. Our three eDSLs are like three quarks
in a nucleon: it’s impossible to separate them because they reference each
other. Cyclic module dependencies are the worst evil of all evils. They are
prohibited in Haskell. This either forces us to have a god-like module or think
about the careful separation of concerns with adopting proper techniques
certainly.

We won’t improve the languages that much in the next section, but we learn a
much-needed design technique.

5.2.4 Decoupling languages with parameterized ADTs
In OOP, we would like to go with OOP interfaces to decouple something
from something. OOP interfaces are mostly dynamic polymorphism.
Statically typed solutions do exist as well, and they often involve plenty of
type-level magic. The Haskell world is densely packed with such things so
that type-level polymorphism dominates over the codebases. Type families
and type classes inhabit any more or less complicated library, so that
decoupling essentially becomes genericity.

This is why the task of decoupling our eDSLs has multiple solutions in
Haskell, but perhaps, we should not jump that high today. Parametrizing
algebraic data types that first come to mind would prepare us for the Free
monadic solutions in the next chapter.

Given that the LogicControl language is the main and top-level, we’ll let it
know about the other two languages. Hdl and DeviceControl eDSLs will be
subordinate. The hierarchical structure of the languages remains the same, but
the two underlying ones won’t know what they nest. This will be a type
parameter so that the outer LogicControl could decide on what continuations
the inner languages should have. Consider listing 5.6 about the parametrized
Hdl type in which I’m presenting the full module. The DeviceControl is
reworked similarly, so I’ll omit it.

Listing 5.6 Parametrized Hdl language

module Andromeda.Hardware.Language.Hdl where

import Andromeda.Hardware.Common (ComponentPassport)
import Andromeda.Hardware.Domain (DeviceName, ControllerName,
  ComponentIndex, Controller)

type Hdl next = [HdlMethod next]

data HdlMethod next
  = SetupController
      DeviceName
      ControllerName
      ComponentPassport
      (Controller -> next)                       #A
  | RegisterComponent Controller ComponentIndex ComponentPassport


It’s the same, except it has an abstract, unknown continuation next. It will be
specified in LogicControlMethod. See listing 5.7.
Listing 5.7 LogicControl specifies inner languages

module Andromeda.LogicControl.Language where

import Andromeda.Common (Key, Value, Message)
import Andromeda.Hardware.Language.Hdl (Hdl)
import Andromeda.Hardware.Language.DeviceControl (DeviceControl)

data LogicControlMethod
  = EvalHdl (Hdl LogicControl)
  | EvalDeviceControl (DeviceControl LogicControl)
  | Report Message
  | Store Key Value

type LogicControl = [LogicControlMethod]

It could be Hdl LogicControlMethod instead of Hdl LogicControl, but I
won’t argue for one or another. Neither option influences the design, just
alters it a little. Let’s update figure 5.4 and show this decoupling by dashed
lines, see figure 5.5:

Figure 5.5 Decoupled languages




This trick does its job, modules become less coupled and better concentrated,
but I don’t think that developer experience of the languages is improved. The
script from listing 5.4 didn’t change at all. So what’s the point?
Hopefully, this reasoning will help you in some cases; it’s an easy way to get
started with generics and parameterized ADTs. Sometimes, we want our
domain models and domain-specific languages to be customized, and
sometimes extracting subparts of a language into separate modules makes
sense on its own. For example, when the inner language is supposed to have a
slightly different lifetime. Or when it’s required in other projects, as a library.
You’ll need to do a little refactoring of your languages, and there is a chance
that the business logic won’t be affected that much.

Yet, I’m not very satisfied. Writing such scripts is too tedious. The root cause
that leads to this design isn’t that obvious. The cause is that our methods are
dissimilar to “normal” functions. Normal functions return values, as they do
in all the programming languages. Instead, our methods have to push result
values deeper into the nested languages, which proceed this plunging to
access the “outer” ones. The continuation-passing style of encoding things
doesn’t work nicely here. Or at least, it’s an implementation detail, an
internal mechanism that leaks to the business logic and makes things too
verbose. And you know what? We’ll fix this issue with Free monads in the
next chapter. Our languages will be decoupled, will be correctly organized,
they will be monadic, and a usual, familiar style of coding will be allowed.

I encourage you to read further. But before we jump to the following topics,
we’d like to pay some attention to the dirty underworld of interpretable
languages—namely, interpreters.

5.3 Implementation of eDSLs
Interpreting isn’t something new to us. It’s been discussed many times
before, in many contexts, with many samples. Conceptually, interpreters for
the languages we’ve just developed are pretty much the same. You take your
ADT structure, pattern-match over it, and connect the steps to the real
actions. We’ve even created a light testing infrastructure with mocking
interpreters that could fake things and let us check our business scripts in
isolation.

I won’t get looping around the same theme again and again, but there is a
topic to discuss more thoroughly: maintaining an internal state of interpreters.
Interestingly, our languages don’t explicitly expose something for stateful
applications, but what are the result values of eDSL methods if not state?
Immutable, localized, yet a state. For example, the ctrl variable in listing 5.5
holds an instance of a controller… Well, some reference to it.
newtype Controller = Controller ControllerName

A logical question goes here: where is the instance then? Don’t we think that
the controller name is everything we need to interact with the actual
controllers? Of course, it’s a bit more complicated. The instance exists, and it
only exists in the runtime, not in the scenarios. It’s time to talk about runtime
objects and their lifetimes.

5.3.1 Runtime objects and lifetimes
The Controller type is just a reference. It’s an domain-level avatar of the
real controller, and it doesn’t even have to be the same on the implementation
level. We only operate by it in scripts, it’s this ctrl variable:
SetupController "device" "ctrl" passp1 (\ctrl -> ...)

There should be another representation explicitly for the implementation
level. Having a Controller value, we indirectly access the internal, runtime
representation. But the latter should never occur in the scripts because it’s an
implementation detail. Runtime instances are addressed by a Controller
value, and have everything needed to query the real controller. We did it
before already, just not for controllers. What is SensorAPI from the previous
chapter if not a low-level interface to sensors? And what is the Device type if
not a runtime instance?

In this chapter, the meaning of the controller has changed: it’s not just a
device part, but it’s something more significant. The languages have been
evolved as well, and now we can talk about the path of Controller values
through the scripts. Consider the ctrl variable from listing 5.5 again. Do you
see how long it should live? It’s only accessible to the methods on the same
level, or on the deeper levels. What should the corresponding instance look
like then? How long should it live? To figure this out, take a look at figure
5.6 first.
Figure 5.6 Script flow and lifetimes of instances




This is essentially a sequence diagram that shows how the scenario from
listing 5.5 flows. The lifetime of ctrl corresponds to the lifetime of its
runtime instance. The ctrl value will be disposed of automatically when the
interpreter leaves the scope of the SetupController method (the second
dashed line). Now we have a choice: either let the instance live after the
script is finished (a dashed continuation of its lifetime on the picture), or
destroy it right after the ctrl becomes inaccessible.

Earlier, a Device value could live longer than the script itself. We’ve been
utilizing it in the tests:
-- hdl script run
device <- makeDevice aaaHardwareService hdl

-- device utilization
mbTherm <- getDevicePart aaaHardwareService "therm" device

Device was a state created by the makeDevice interpreting function.
However, the assumption that a Hdl script describes a single device is not
quite accurate and doesn’t work with the new languages where a controller is
a boss, and it is it who determines a device itself. When there is no controller
there is no device visible to the central computer. We should rework this part
of the implementation accordingly.

Below goes the type for controller instances called ControllerImpl. It has
two fields: a name and vendor-related data, and it doesn’t hold any controller-
specific possibly-mutable state for now.
data ControllerImpl = ControllerImpl ControllerName VendorComponent

Let’s update the Device machinery. To remove unnecessary obstacles when
accessing the controller, we put ControllerImpl right into Device:
data Device = Device DeviceName ControllerImpl DeviceParts

A controller instance or maybe other device parts actually should have a
state. A lot of things can happen to devices in the network. Controller
instances may be seen as individual actors, forming the actor model,
behaving somehow, managing their state, and making queries to the real
controllers. Hopefully, our current design will not interfere with this topic
later, in chapter 7, when we’ll be developing the simulator.

As long as it’s possible to produce many devices, our new runtime state will
be a map of devices addressed by a controller. And, for the clarity of the
interpreters, we’ll put devices into the Runtime structure:
type Devices = Map Controller (ControllerImpl, Device)

data Runtime = Runtime
  { _devices         :: Devices
  , _hardwareService :: HardwareService
  }

This is it. This Runtime structure will exist only on the implementation level,
and the interpreters will do their job by interacting with it.
The last thing to discuss in this chapter should be interpreters.

5.3.2 Impure stateful interpreters

The makeDevice function was our interpreter, but this is not enough anymore.
The languages have been improved a lot. We have three organized
hierarchically (as figure 5.5 shows), so the interpreters should be properly
named. Each language will get an interpreter, namely a pair of functions like
runLanguage and interpretLanguageMethod. We’ll start from one for
LogicControl. This is the entry point to interpret all the scripts:

runLogicControl :: Runtime -> LogicControl -> IO Runtime
runLogicControl runtime [] = pure runtime
runLogicControl runtime (m:ms) = do
  runtime' <- interpretLogicControlMethod runtime m
  runLogicControl runtime' ms

Notice that we treat Runtime as an argument-passing state. The function takes
it, traverses the methods recursively, and returns the modified state.

The method interpreting function that is shown below has a couple of
interesting things. First, it calls the other two interpreters: runHdl and
runDeviceControl, and second, it passes this interpreter into them as an
argument.
interpretLogicControlMethod :: Runtime -> LogicControlMethod                 -> IO Runtime

interpretLogicControlMethod runtime (EvalHdl hdl) =
  -- run Hdl script, pass this interpreter deeper
  runHdl runtime runLogicControl hdl

interpretLogicControlMethod runtime (EvalDeviceControl dc) =
  -- run DeviceControl script, pass this interpreter deeper
  runDeviceControl runtime runLogicControl dc

interpretLogicControlMethod runtime (Report msg) = do
  putStrLn msg
  pure runtime

interpretLogicControlMethod runtime (Store k v) = do
  error "not implemented"
It’s not hard to guess why runDeviceControl needs runLogicControl. Our
languages are nested, and moreover, they are parametrized by LogicControl.
The internal interpreters should know how to run the nested LogicControl
scripts, therefore we’re passing runLogicControl to them.

There is no need to investigate the interpreters’ code because it doesn’t give
us any new ideas. You can always consult with the code samples. But I think
you’d want to take a look at the function signatures. They are almost the
same. As the languages are parametrized by something, so the interpreters
are, and this is why they take a generalized external interpreter:
runHdl
  :: Runtime                                  -- input state
  -> (Runtime -> next -> IO Runtime)          -- external interpreter
  -> Hdl next                                 -- script
  -> IO Runtime

runDeviceControl
  :: Runtime
  -> (Runtime -> next -> IO Runtime)
  -> DeviceControl next
  -> IO Runtime

See this repetitive Runtime entry? This might be a problem, and handling a
mutable state as an argument-passing state isn’t that convenient. We can rely
on the fact that we’re on the implementation level after all, and everything
happens in IO, so why not just use mutable reference? Either put the very
Runtime structure into IORef or do this with its fields separately. The second
option is better:
data Runtime = Runtime
  { _devicesRef         :: IORef Devices
  , _hardwareServiceRef :: IORef HardwareService
  }

The interpreters will change slightly. Good thing that their signatures become
shorter and clearer:
interpretLogicControlMethod
  :: Runtime -> LogicControlMethod         -> IO ()

runLogicControl :: Runtime -> LogicControl -> IO ()
runHdl
  :: Runtime
  -> (next -> IO ())         -- no need to pass Runtime anymore
  -> Hdl next
  -> IO Runtime

runDeviceControl
  :: Runtime
  -> (next -> IO ())
  -> DeviceControl next
  -> IO Runtime

This is not the only win we get. We’ll come to behave systems very soon
concurrently. We organize the runtime structures into concurrent mutable
primitives such as MVar and STM TVar instead of thread-unsafe IORef.

I hope you’re finding it entertaining to follow my steps and collect the pearls
I left for you. I believe the next chapter will give you even more of that.

5.4 Summary
     Everything in programming that is unified by a single domain can be
     considered a DSL.
     Domain modeling with algebraic data types helps us to build precise,
     reliable, and understandable domain-specific languages.
     Embedded DSLs are a great tool for analyzing and modeling business
     domains.
     Use interpretable embedded DSLs to tame the complexity of your
     difficult domain and its notions.
     Keep your embedded DSLs properly organized: group methods by their
     meaning and scope, carefully separate responsibilities, and put the
     eDSLs into hierarchies. This will have a good impact on the design of
     the application.
     Runtime objects can have their lifetimes. This helps not to mix the
     notions of the domain with implementations of this domain.
     Later on, separation to eDSLs and implementation will give us even
     more freedom of making the code better.
6 Domain modeling with Free
monads
This chapter covers
      Using Free monads to model business domains
      How to apply the Functor abstraction
      Getting familiar with some advanced type-level concepts such as
      GADTs

If you ask me, lifetimes are what distinguishes interface-like abstractions
from genericity-like ones, such as functors, monoids, categories. There are
many ways to generalize a data structure, but none of them bother about
lifetimes. Generalizations don’t have a dimension of a lifetime because they
are just timeless expressions over data structures. Generalizations focus on
some properties and ignore others, they exhibit a math nature of a structure,
but they don’t “work” in a common way. They rather “declare”. They just
exist on the design time and on the compile time and mostly disappear after
the program is compiled.

I would also state that only interface-like abstractions help us to handle
complexity. Unlike generalizations, interfaces carry some meaning from a
business domain, and their purpose is to give us the minimum of information
needed for the tasks. No implementation details, nothing unrelated, nothing
too abstract. Interfaces communicate the essence of that domain, and often
they serve as documentation. Knowing your interface means knowing a part
of your business domain, at least its public representation.

Figure 6.1 summarized the said:

Figure 6.1 Two sorts of abstractions
TIP


You can get more insights about this dualism from the following talk by
Vitaly Bragilevsky: The clear path to Haskell complexities
https://youtu.be/n3H_YipBDrY

Interfaces are very domain-specific. We’ve already seen how interpretable
eDSLs can be functional interfaces, and we’ve discussed some other
approaches as well (Service Handle and type classes). Now we should ask the
question: What will be the closest analog of OOP interfaces in FP? Which of
them?

And my answer goes below.

6.1 Free monads overview
For some definite reasons, I consider Free monads the most idiomatic
functional interface of all. I found many interesting parallels of them with
OOP interfaces, and this discovery allowed me to utilize the same reasoning I
already had from Object-Oriented Design with C#. Of course, when
comparing OOP and FP on the programming language level, we see it’s
rather a dissimilar story, but on the design level, both functional and OOP
interfaces have the same purpose.

When I realized this, everything I knew from the mainstream software design
started playing its role: design principles, code quality metrics, project
organization ideas. My FDD methodology is by the way offers Free monads
as a key design pattern. You may certainly do other things instead: Final
Tagless, effect systems, the ReaderT pattern, but I’m very confident that Free
monads do it better. I wouldn’t be saying this if I haven’t been succeeding
with them in practice. But it was definitely a success. I’ve adopted Free
monads in real production in several companies, I’ve designed five (yes, five)
frameworks for various purposes, including four commercial ones. This
allowed us to achieve our goals in time, on budget, and the business of my
employers benefited from this work very much.

I dedicated the second half of this book to programming such a framework.
All the tasks we usually meet in the real work will emerge here: relational
and key-value databases, logging, concurrency, REST applications structure,
and so forth. Free monads will be used as an architectural design pattern to
achieve testability, separation of concerns, clarity, and other good properties
of software.

This section will give you an overview of the Free monad pattern. The 6.2
section should be enough for you to start building your own eDSLs with Free
monads. I’ll also give you some technical background on how this
mechanism works, and what’s inside. My final goal will be to prove to you
that there is a smooth shift from what we’ve already done with interpretable
ADTs to a more powerful and more convenient way of designing eDSLs.

6.1.1 The Free monad pattern
The Free monad, which comes from the depths of category theory, is used to
wrap some (but not all) types into a monad. Applications of the Free monad
usually live in the academic part of functional programming, but there is one
important functional pattern we can adopt in practical development. The Free
monad pattern helps you to separate subsystem interface from subsystem
implementation and provide an eDSL that your clients can use to describe
scenarios for your subsystem without knowing the implementation details. In
this case, an eDSL will be a free monadic language that reflects the logic of
your subsystem in a safe, concise way.

There are three key points of this pattern:

     declarative Free monad eDSL;
     declarative Free monadic scenarios of one’s business logic and domain;
     Free interpreters to sew the declarations and implementations.

All the points will be discussed further, after the following scheme of the
pattern (figure 6.2):

Figure 6.2 The Free monad pattern




Technically speaking, the Free monad is a monad. On its own, this Free
monadic mechanism doesn’t express anything about particular domains, it’s
rather a template to wrap over your eDSLs. Putting them into the Free monad
will make them monadic, sequential. It’s much better than being list-based.
Sequences with lists have too much boilerplate and are quite limited in
different aspects. In Haskell and Scala, “monadic” also means that the do-
notation (for-comprehension) becomes available to your scenarios, and tons
of boilerplate will just go away.

Let’s get a sense of the pattern by making a better version of the Hdl
language (yes, again). What if I tell you that this list-based and continuation-
powered script we’ve seen previously (listing 6.1) can be done much more
readable while being monadic (listing 6.2)?

Listing 6.1 Continuation-powered and list-based Hdl script

boostersDef :: Hdl (Hdl ())
boostersDef =
  [ SetupController "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passport
    ( \lCtrl ->
      [ RegisterComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
      , RegisterComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-p" aaaPressure02Passport
      ]
    )
  , SetupController "right booster" "right b ctrl" aaaController86Passport
    ( \rCtrl ->
      [ RegisterComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
      , RegisterComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-p" aaaPressure02Passport
      ]
    )
  ]

Listing 6.2 Free monad-based monadic Hdl script

createBoosters :: Hdl (Controller, Controller)
createBoosters = do
  lCtrl <- setupController "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passp
  registerComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
  registerComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-p" aaaPressure02Passport

  rCtrl <- setupController "right booster" "right b ctrl" aaaController86Pas
  registerComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
  registerComponent rCtrl "nozzle2-p" aaaPressure02Passport
  pure (lCtrl, rCtrl)

I would hightlight several important points here.
     The bosstersDef script doesn’t return anything. The consumer of this
     script won’t be able to access the handles of the controllers because they
     are enclosed internally, within continuation lambdas (lCtrl and rCtrl
     variables).
     In contrast, the createBoosters script is capable of returning things.
     This time we give control over the just created devices to the client code.
     The script returns both controllers, but actually, it can return anything
     you want because it’s a normal Haskell function.
     You can read the second script naturally. It’s not polluted by
     unnecessary list brackets and is plain. At least, we see it as plain, and
     we’ll see that internally, it has the same continuations and chains as the
     original code, just better organized with the Free monad.

Both scripts share the same property: they are interpretable, not immediately
evaluable. Free monads work similarly because they are interpretable
algebraic data types. In fact, we’ve come close to inventing this mechanism
when we're talking about continuations. Let me show you the Free-prepared
Hdl ADT, and please do not be afraid of the new bits:

data HdlMethod next

  = SetupController
       DeviceName
       ControllerName
       ComponentPassport
       (Controller -> next)

  | RegisterComponent
       Controller
       ComponentIndex
       ComponentPassport
       (() -> next)

Hmm, well… There are no new bits actually. It’s the same ADT, almost. You
won’t be scared by that strange continuation (() -> next) in the
RegisterComponent method, right? The Free monad pattern requires all the
methods to have continuations. Also, we have to make our ADT a Functor
for reasons. Just follow the template for now: create a Functor type class
instance, pattern-match over the ADT, and transform all the continuations
with the Functor’s function:
instance Functor HdlMethod where

  fmap f (SetupController deviceName ctrlName passp next) =
     SetupController deviceName ctrlName passp (f . next)

  fmap f (RegisterComponent controller idx passp next) =
     RegisterComponent controller idx passp (f . next)

What are those (f . next) applications? Why this fmap function is needed?
A detailed explanation will come very soon, among other implementation
details. We’d want to see the overview of the pattern at this moment.

The HdlMethod ADT is ready to be wrapped by the Free monad. Luckily, it
has everything required: the continuation fields, and the type parameter.
Notice how the HdlMethod type is partially applied here, kind of:
type Hdl a = Free HdlMethod a

The Free type has this definition, it’s an ADT that is twice parametrized and
also is recursive. Not a simple one:
data Free t a
  = Pure a
  | Free (t (Free t a))

The HdlMethod substitutes the first t parameter, and the rest of this code is
clearly obvious… Don’t you agree?

From this moment, the do-notation at your disposal, as it’s shown in listing
6.2, but maybe you’d want to have a couple of smart constructors for a better
developer experience. Those ones:
setupController :: DeviceName -> ControllerName -> ComponentPassport -> Hdl
setupController deviceName ctrlName passp =
  Free (SetupController deviceName ctrlName passp Pure)

registerComponent :: Controller -> ComponentIndex -> ComponentPassport -> Hd
registerComponent controller idx passp =
  Free (RegisterComponent controller idx passp Pure)

I know it’s all a bit cryptic: the Free constructor engulfing the domain
constructor, the Pure one that is placed as a continuation… There is a
technical story behind the scenes, and I’ll explain everything in the following
sections. As well as we’ll talk about Free interpreters - the missing piece in
this pattern overview. But before indeed unrolling this ravel, I’d want to
dissertate on Free monad scripts in comparison to IO scripts.

6.1.2 Free monads versus IO
Remember there was a “returning” problem in the naive design based on
algebraic data types? We had to nest the languages and chain scripts of
different types by means of continuations. Paradoxically, Free monads also
do this kind of nesting, but at the same time, they resolve the “returning”
problem and turn the scripts into a more usual form. You can easily compare
the code in listings 6.1 and 6.2 and see the difference. Just a small sample
here:
-- ADT-based script:

boosterDef :: Hdl (Hdl ())
boosterDef =
  [ SetupController "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passport
    ( \lCtrl -> [ RegisterComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passpo
  ]

-- Free-monad based script:

createBooster :: Hdl Controller
createBooster = do
  lCtrl <- setupController "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Passp
  registerComponent lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
  pure lCtrl

In practice, this makes a huge difference. Usually, in Haskell and Scala,
business logic developers prefer regular everyday normal functions instead of
trees of ADTs. I won’t be probably wrong if I say that most of the codebases
don’t even try to keep business logic pure, separated from the real IO. Most
of the codebases work in the IO instantly, and such code isn’t interpretable,
it’s immediately evaluable. An IO counterpart to the createBoosters
function will be this:
createBoostersIO :: IO Controller
createBoostersIO = do
  lCtrl <- setupControllerIO "left booster" "left b ctrl" aaaController86Pas
  registerComponentIO lCtrl "nozzle1-t" aaaTemperature25Passport
  pure lCtrl

setupControllerIO :: DeviceName -> ControllerName -> ComponentPassport -> IO

registerComponentIO :: Controller -> ComponentIndex -> ComponentPassport ->

While there is a surface, syntactic similarity between the Free monadic and
IO functions, the real difference is huge. In fact, the IO functions aren’t
complete here. There should be vendor-related bits for creating devices.
Previously, they were separated from the ADT scripts and moved into the
implementation layer. But where they are in the createBoostersIO,
setupControllerIO, and registerComponentIO functions? Are they just
baked into the scripts directly, introducing a tight, unwanted dependency
from implementation details?
import Andromeda.Assets.Vendors.AAA.HardwareService (aaaHardwareService)

getHardwareService :: IO HardwareService
getHardwareService = pure aaaHardwareService

setupControllerIO :: DeviceName -> ControllerName -> ComponentPassport -> IO
setupControllerIO deviceName ctrlName passp = do

  service <- getHardwareService
  eCtrl <- makeController service ctrlName passp
  ...

Software developers should know why this code has a bad smell. It was
found long ago that such a naive straightforward code will most likely
become a problem in the nearest future. It’s barely testable, it has wrong
dependencies, it has extra knowledge about things it should not know about.
A common refactoring will be Dependency Injection, thankfully we have
interfaces for the hardware service already:
createBoostersIO :: HardwareService -> IO Controller
setupControllerIO :: HardwareService -> DeviceName -> ControllerName -> Comp

registerComponentIO :: HardwareService -> Controller -> ComponentIndex -> Co

Also, there might be a need for some state or some stateful environment, and
the signatures of the functions start growing:
createBoostersIO :: HardwareService -> IORef SomeState -> IO Controller

Or maybe, with the ReaderT pattern:
createBoostersIO :: HardwareService -> ReaderT (IORef SomeState) IO Controll

Dependency Injection helps a little, but it can’t solve the main problem:
we’ve mixed two layers here. A layer of business domain and the
implementation layer. Moreover, the IO is by itself a big danger. With IO,
bad things are sneaking into the code like intruders, and you’ll never know
when everything explodes. If something bad can happen - it will happen most
likely.

With Free monads, however, all these problems just disappear. The scrict,
inavoidable division of the pure domain and impure implemenation makes it
very hard to violate the border between layers. The complexity of business
logic scripts becomes lower due to removing the unrelated implementation
details from them. Scripts do look imperative as usual, but internally, it’s an
interpretable tree of ADTs which is declarative by nature. If you’re interested
where the SomeState goes, the story with state is also good. This is a very
important topic, so we’ll discuss it in the next chapters. As well as testability.

6.2 How Free monads work
I find it beneficial to have a thorough lesson on the functional programming
concepts coming with the Free monad approach. This is a step down from the
design level to the implementation level, and the tutorial here contains a lot of
things about the internal, usually hidden mechanisms. I hope it will give you
enough understanding so that we could talk about Free monads freely in
further.

LINK


To get a deeper explanation, you might want to read the excellent tutorial
“Why Free Monads Matter,” by Gabriel Gonzalez.
https://www.haskellforall.com/2012/06/you-could-have-invented-free-
monads.html
6.2.1 Wrapping languages into the Free monad

In a mathematical sense, a Free monadic language can be built over any type
that is a Functor. If you have such a type that’s an algebra of your domain,
all you should do to make it monadic is declare a Functor for it and then
declare a Free monad over that functor. If that’s okay, forget this
mathematical introduction, because we’re about to do it the practical way.

A running sample will be an eDSL for making sandwiches. We all love
snacking on sandwiches! Why not memorize our favorite recipes. My
sandwich is quite simple:
mySandwich :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich = do
  body1 <- startNewSandwich Toast Tomato
  body2 <- addComponent Cheese body1
  body3 <- addComponent Salt body2
  finishSandwich (Just Toast) body3

I’m sure, you can read this script because it’s self-descriptive. You don’t
even need to see the definition of types for Toast, Tomato, Sandwich,
Cheese, and Salt, to get the meaning. You’ll need those types in order to
construct your own sandwiches so here they are, shortened for saving space:
data BreadType = Baguette | Toast
data Component = Bread BreadType | Tomato | Salt | Cheese
data SandwichBody = SandwichBody BreadType [Component]
data Sandwich = Sandwich BreadType (Maybe BreadType) [Component]

What if we incidentally confuse body1, body2, and body3? Given that the
script is monadic, it can be shortened and freed from that probable mistake:
mySandwich2 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich2
    = startNewSandwich Toast Tomato
  >>= addComponent Cheese
  >>= addComponent Salt
  >>= finishSandwich (Just Toast)

It’s a sequential recipe, and everything we know about the result is that there
will be a Sandwich. The bind operator here (>>=) replaces the left arrow of
the do-notation (<-). All the functions addComponent and finishSandwich
are partially applied and so they don’t require to specify those body
arguments. In other words, all the functions are lambdas with one argument.
Explicit writing of the same script shows that, and also demonstrates that
continuations play a significant role in the monadic code:
mySandwich3 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich3
    = startNewSandwich Toast Tomato
    >>= (\body1 -> addComponent Cheese body1
        >>= (\body2 -> addComponent Salt body2
            >>= (\body3 -> finishSandwich (Just Toast) body3
                )
            )
        )

It’s not yet clear what types body1, body2 and body3 have. Or, similarly, what
are the signatures of the constructing functions. This must be important for
the gourmet who wants to compose a snack. Among the Free monadic type
SandwichRecipe, these functions form a handly eDSL. Listing 6.3 contains
the full definition of it, excluding the Functor instance. Look at the methods
StartNewSandwich, StartNewSandwich, and FinishSandwich with their
continuations, and see how the return types in those continuations match to
the return types of smart constructors:

Listing 6.3 The SandwichRecipe Free monad language

data SandwichConstructor next

  = StartNewSandwich
      BreadType
      Component
      (SandwichBody -> next)                  -- continuation, getting
                                              -- SandwichBody as a result
  | AddComponent
      Component
      SandwichBody
      (SandwichBody -> next)                  -- continuation, getting
                                              -- SandwichBody as a result
  | FinishSandwich
      (Maybe BreadType)
      SandwichBody
      (Sandwich -> next)                      -- continuation, getting
                                          -- Sandwich as a result

type SandwichRecipe a = Free SandwichConstructor a


startNewSandwich
  :: BreadType
  -> Component
  -> SandwichRecipe SandwichBody                                   #A
startNewSandwich breadType component
  = Free (StartNewSandwich breadType component Pure)               #B

addComponent
  :: Component
  -> SandwichBody
  -> SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
addComponent component sandwichBody
  = Free (AddComponent component sandwichBody Pure)

finishSandwich
  :: Maybe BreadType     -- the last piece of bread is optional
  -> SandwichBody
  -> SandwichRecipe Sandwich
finishSandwich mbBreadType sandwichBody
  = Free (FinishSandwich mbBreadType sandwichBody Pure)


It’s only the Free type that is a monad and that can be used in the do-
notation. We can’t write the following:
mySandwich4 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich4 = do
  incomplete <- StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato ???????
  finishSandwich (Just Toast) incomplete

This won’t compile. And what is the continuation should be instead of the
question marks? We have to wrap the domain constructor into the Free type
manually, knowing its recursive structure. Not by chance, the Free type itself
has the two special constructors:
data Free t a
  = Pure a                 -- Termination case for recursive nesting
  | Free                   -- Recursive nesting of the language
     (t                    -- Algebra of the language
        (Free t a)         -- The nested language in the Free form
      )

     Pure. This (monadic) value finalizes the chain of computations. It also
     keeps some value of type a to be returned after the interpretation is over.
     Free. Action (monadic) that should be able to keep another action of
     type Free a inside. We call the other action a continuation because
     when the outer action is interpreted, the process of interpretation should
     be continued with the inner action.

And not by chance it’s recursive. The first constructor is needed for
terminating a monadic chain, and the second one wraps around a domain
method with its continuations. The correct manual wrapping should look like
this:
mySandwich4 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich4 = do
  incomplete <- Free
    ( StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato Pure
    )
  FinishSandwich (Just Toast) incomplete Pure

Still, manual wrapping doesn’t look as clean as with the smart constructors.
mySandwich4 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich4 = do
  incomplete <- startNewSandwich Toast Tomato
  finishSandwich (Just Toast) incomplete

Later on, when we do the binding, this dummy Pure continuation will go
through all the circles of hell and will end up nowhere. The Free monad
binding mechanism (that one we didn’t see yet), and the Functor mechanism
all together will put our domain constructors into a self-nested, self-recursion
chain. We don’t have to bother about this as script writers. You might want to
make an exercise and figure out the unwrapped version of the mySandwich
script. I highly advise you to practice it before moving further!

6.2.2 The Functor instance for the Free monad
We just started falling into the white rabbit hole. Things are flickering before
our eyes, and you’re clearly recognizing one on the wall: a Functor instance
(listing 6.4):

Listing 6.4 The Functor instance for the Free monad

instance Functor SandwichConstructor where

  fmap f (StartNewSandwich breadType component next)
    = StartNewSandwich breadType component (f . next)

  fmap f (AddComponent component sandwichBody next)
    = AddComponent component sandwichBody (f . next)

  fmap f (FinishSandwich mbBreadType sandwichBody next)
    = FinishSandwich mbBreadType sandwichBody (f . next)

What does it do? Let’s talk about Functors a little bit.

Type is a Functor when you can map some function f over its internals
without changing the data structure. A plain list is a functor because you can
map many functions over its internals (items), while the structure of the list
remains the same. For example,
oldList :: [Int]
oldList = [1, 2, 3, 4]

newList :: [String]
newList = map show oldList

-- newList: ["1", "2", "3", "4"]

When you call the map function, you actually use the list type as a Functor.
You may use the fmap function instead — the only method of the Functor
type class:
newList' = fmap show list

This is the same. Consider the type class Functor and its instance for the list
type:
class Functor f where
  fmap :: (a -> b) -> f a -> f b

instance Functor [] where
  fmap = map

Here, f is [] (list), the a type variable is Int, and the b type variable is
String:

fmap :: (Int -> String) -> [Int] -> [String]

By mapping the (show :: Int -> String) function over the list, we get a
list of the same size. The structure of the data doesn’t change, but the internal
type may be different. Both oldList and newList are lists, but they carry
items of different types: Int and String.

Now we can declare the Functor instance for the SandwichConstructor
type. The fmap function will be specified like this:
fmap
  :: (a -> b)
  -> SandwichConstructor a
  -> SandwichConstructor b

Consequently, our type should have a type parameter for some purpose.
Why? The type SandwichConstructor a has two value constructors:
StartNewSandwich, AddComponent and FinishSandwich. All of them have
fields with parameter type a, all of them are mappable. From listing 6.4, you
can see that the methods are being repacked, with the continuation reiterated.
The new continuation does what the old was doing, but also the result of the
old one will be transformed with the f function. This is what the Functor
instance is for: to alter the internals without changing the shape.

In the case of Free monads, the fmap function will be altering the type of
continuations when it comes to merging two Free monadic scripts. The
procedure of replacing continuations looks by the way very similar to the
way two usual lists are concatenating. The Free monad traverses a script from
the top outer action to the inner one. Then, the Free monad replaces the
innermost continuation with the second script.
script1 :: SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
script1 = startNewSandwich Toast Tomato

script2 :: SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
script2 =
  script1                                  -- traversing script1 and
  >>=                                      -- replacing the continuation
  addComponent Salt                        -- happens here

scirpt3 :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
scirpt3 = do
  b <- script2                     -- traversing a longer chain
  finishSandwich (Just Toast) b    -- consisting of script1
                                   -- and script2

And since this traversing happens every time we call the bind (>>=) function,
the usual Free monad has an O(n^2) complexity of biding. Yes, quadratic,
exactly as it is for lists concatenation. So the longer your scripts, the slower
they will work. But don’t worry, this is a simple Free monad, it’s not the only
one. There are various Free monads that don’t have this problem of quadratic
complexity, for example, the so-called Church-Encoded Free monad. Moving
from Free to Church-Encoded Free won’t take much time. You’d need to
change a few lines in your code, and - wow, it works better now. Doesn’t
matter what are the internals, and how the bind function is implemented in
both Free monads, just treat them as a black box, a good tool to create
monadic eDSLs.

TIP


The “no remorse” Free monad from PureScript has a fairly fast
implementation. It’s called so because it’s based on the paper “Reflection
without remorse”. The free Haskell library by Edward Kmett contains both
the usual Free monad (the Free type) and the Church-Encoded Free monad,
the F type. This one is really fast. Going ahead, its performance is on par with
Final Tagless.

LINK


Reflection without remorse http://okmij.org/ftp/Haskell/zseq.pdf

TIP


The Hydra showcase framework has two engines: based on the usual Free
monad and based on the Church-encoded Free monad. You can compare the
performance of the two by auditing the same application written on top of
them.

LINK


The Hydra project https://github.com/graninas/Hydra

6.2.3 Learning the recursive Free type
A better intuition for Free monads may come from comprehending of why
this type is recursive, and what is the problem it solves. What happens when
we parametrize it?
type SandwichRecipe a = Free SandwichConstructor a

Substituting the t parameter manually in the Free ADT will give the
following:
data Free t a
  = Pure a
  | Free (t (Free t a))

=>

Free SandwichConstructor a
  = Pure a
  | Free (SandwichConstructor (Free SandwichConstructor a))

The bind function (>>=) uses fmap to nest values and push the bottom Pure
value deeper in the recursive chain. If you’re wondering how it’s
implemented in Edward Kmett’s library free, then look:
instance Functor t => Monad (Free t) where
  return = pure
  Pure a >>= f = f a
  Free m >>= f = Free ((>>= f) <$> m)

I’ll leave it to your consideration. What I want to tell instead, is how this
recursive Free type eliminates the uncontrolled growing of type signatures.
With a naive nesting of scripts, you’d get something like this:
SandwichConstructor (SandwichConstructor (SandwichConstructor SandwichBody))

The Free type doesn’t have this problem. All the scripts are of the same type
SandwichRecipe a, no matter how deep they are. Let’s elaborate on this.

Putting SandwichConstructor a into itself will give the following:
val' = \b -> AddComponent Cheese b (\b' -> ())
val'' = \b -> AddComponent Salt b val'
val = StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato val''
or else:
val = StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato
       (\b1 -> AddComponent Salt b1
          (\b2 -> AddComponent Cheese b2 (\b3 -> ()))
       )

The more actions we insert, the bigger the type of the action becomes:
val' :: SandwichBody -> SandwichConstructor ()
val'' :: SandwichBody -> SandwichConstructor (SandwichConstructor ())
val :: SandwichConstructor (SandwichConstructor (SandwichConstructor ()))

That is, all three variables val', val'', and val have different types
(ignoring the SandwichBody argument), and we can't use them uniformly. The
Free type solves this problem by wrapping our enhanced type with its own
recursive structures. Because of this, the nesting of values of type Free
SandwichConstructor a instead of just SandwichConstructor a gives a
unified type for the whole chain regardless of its length:
val' :: SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
val' = Free (StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato Pure)

val'' :: SandwichBody -> SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
val'' = \body -> Free (AddComponent Salt body Pure)

val :: SandwichRecipe SandwichBody
val = val' >>= val''

A real work of the binding mechanism can be illustrated in pseudocode:
val = val' >>= val''

=>
bind
  (Free (StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato Pure))
  (\body -> Free (AddComponent Salt body Pure))

=>

val = (Free (StartNewSandwich Toast Tomato
              (\body -> Free (AddComponent Salt body Pure))
          )
      )

It’s a bit more difficult to explain why the crafty recursive Free type reduces
everything to a single type. But it really does. It acts as a Russian nesting doll
and does so on both levels: on the value level, and on the level of types.

Worth noting that recursion is the only fundamental way to chain
computations in functional programming, upon which all other concepts are
based. Not continuations. Not monads. Recursion. It doesn't matter whether
we mean recursive functions or types, continuations, or another thing that has
a self-repeating property. Essentially, the Free type represents the fixed point
combinator. In turn, the fixed point combinator is a fundamental
representation of recursion in lambda calculus.

But don’t pay that much attention to this theoretical fact, because there is a
more important thing to talk about. To give the Free monad even more sense,
we should talk about Free interpreters.

6.2.4 Interpretation of Free monadic scripts
There will be no information conceptually new to us here because we’re
familiar with interpretation very well. But looking through the prism of the
Free type described above, we can suppose that Free interpreters are a bit
trickier. Frankly, I don’t think they are, because there is a single common
template for all the Free interpreters that you can just copy-paste for all your
eDSLs. One can say the code of those interpreters is boilerplate because its
structure doesn’t change that much. I’d rather call it a pattern, not boilerplate.

In some sense, Free monad interpreters are more straightforward than ones
we’ve created for custom mutually-recursive eDSLs in the previous chapter.
As figure 6.3 says, we have to unroll both the Free type and our domain-
specific type and match all the methods with real actions.

Figure 6.3 Unrolling a Free script




Unrolling here effectively means pattern-matching the Free value
constructor, descending into its continuation field, then pattern-matching a
domain-specific constructor, then descending into its continuation field, and
repeating those steps again until the end - the Pure value constructor - is
reached. In listing 6.5, a schematic interpreting mechanism is built of two
functions: interpretRecipe and interpretStep.

Listing 6.5 Schematic interpreter

interpretRecipe
  :: IORef Ingredients
  -> SandwichRecipe Sandwich
  -> IO Sandwich

-- Finishing the interpretation process with a result:
interpretRecipe ingredsRef (Pure sandwich) = pure sandwich

-- Recursive interpretation:
interpretRecipe ingredsRef (Free step) = do
  next <- interpretStep ingredsRef step                      -- interpret
  interpretRecipe ingredsRef next                            -- next step

interpretStep
  :: IORef Ingredients
  -> SandwichConstructor a                   -- step
  -> IO a                                    -- result of a step
interpretStep ingredsRef (StartNewSandwich _ _ _) = ...
interpretStep ingredsRef (AddComponent _ _ _)     = ...
interpretStep ingredsRef (FinishSandwich _ _ _)   = ...

For the sake of simplicity, this interpreter made stateful, namely dealing with
an IORef. The Ingredients type will only exist at the interpretation layer,
it’s an implementation detail and should not occur in the business logic. This
type denotes a real number of ingredients available in the kitchen:
type Ingredients = Map Component Int

The interpretStep function does all the things needed to bookkeep the
ingredients and to construct a sandwich. It doesn’t do any other effects except
reading and writing IORef. For example, the AddComponent method is
processed:
interpretStep ingredsRef (AddComponent component body next) = do

  -- update the ingredients
  withdrawIngredient ingredsRef component

  -- reconstruct the snack
  let SandwichBody bread components = body
  let body' = SandwichBody bread (component : components)

  -- return the result, with the continuation
  pure (next body')


-- Updating the actual number of ingredients.
-- Throws exception on error.
withdrawIngredient :: IORef Ingredients -> Component -> IO ()
withdrawIngredient ingredsRef component = do
  ingreds <- readIORef ingredsRef
  case Map.lookup component ingreds of
    Nothing -> error ("Ingredient not found: " <> show component)
    Just count | count <= 0 -> error ("No ingredient: " <> show component)
                | otherwise -> do
       let ingreds' = Map.insert component (count - 1) ingreds
       writeIORef ingredsRef ingreds'

Notice the important thing about the next continuation field. In each method,
we should obtain the result of the method and feed the continuation field. But
the interpretStep function doesn’t go deeper into the recursion, it returns
the continuation back to the caller. It’s an interesting mechanic that is not
really obvious from first sight. It also makes it possible for the
interpretStep function to return a polymorphic type IO a whatever it is. It
can’t be everything though, this type variable a is defined for each domain
method of the SandwichConstructor type. For AddComponent, a continuation
field next expects SandwichBody. We feed this next field by a new sandwich
body and exit the interpretStep function. If you put everything you know
about continuation fields and the Free type, you’ll see that for this case, the
type becomes:
interpretStep
  :: IORef Ingredients
  -> SandwichConstructor (SandwichRecipe SandwichBody)
  -> IO (SandwichRecipe SandwichBody)

I could make this particular interpreter pure (not IO), by just reconstructing
the Ingredients dictionary on every step, without any mutable references,
but the code will be much more verbose compared to IORef. I could make the
interpreter much safer and process errors in a better way. For now, it throws
an exception if some ingredient is lacking, but this isn’t how real systems
should be designed. In Haskell, the error function is mostly a tool for
debugging and should be avoided if possible. In further, we’ll learn a lot
about stateful interpreters and error processing strategies, starting from the
next chapter. State in functional programs becomes a very important object to
think about, and we have to design it carefully.

6.3 Free monadic eDSLs
Many topics have been covered already: requirements analysis, domain-
specific versus domain-agnostic languages, ADTs for domain modeling,
layering, continuations, declarative scripts, and simulating imperativity by
means of interpretable eDSLs, distancing from the impure world and the
benefits of being pure. We definitely missed even more topics. Moreover,
there are millions of ideas in domain-driven design, all are interesting and
enlightening. Who will be the first to write a book on it?

This talk on Free monadic eDSLs will be the last conversation on domain
modeling. We’ll implement a better version of the scripting languages, and
will try to chart the way to the rest of the spaceship control system, namely,
spaceship schema, network, and environment. My goal is to show how
convenient monadic scripting can be, and how much benefits it gives to
business logic programmers.

6.3.1 Redesigning eDSLs with Free monads
Keeping the overall hierarchical structure of the languages the same, we’ll
decouple them completely. With the “returning” feature of Free monads, the
problem we faced in the previous chapter, is no longer actual. The overall
development experience will be much better now.

You’ve already seen the renewed Free monadic Hdl script in listing 6.2. It’s
the createBoosters function with this signature:
createBoosters :: Hdl (Controller, Controller)

This function composes with other Hdl functions easily as all of them share
the same monad: Hdl. This will be a correct yet not that meaningful code:
createBoostersTwice :: Hdl ()
createBoostersTwice = do
  (c1, c2) <- createBoosters
  (c3, c4) <- createBoosters
  -- do nothing here, forget c1, c2, c3, c4
  pure ()

With that, we can think of the LogicControl language as it was Free monadic
too. Reworking its old ADT should be quite straightforward for the two
methods Report and Store. The old ADT was this:
data LogicControlMethod
  = Report Message
  | Store Key Value
  ...

Neither of the two actions returns a value currently, we don’t even wait for
these methods to indicate success or failure. But freemonadization will
require adding continuation fields anyway. They will just explicitly accept ()
- an empty value. A bunch of other boilerplate things should be specified:
data LogicControlMethod next
  = Report Message (() -> next)
  | Store Key Value (() -> next)

instance Functor LogicControlMethod where
  fmap f (Report msg next)      = Report msg      (f . next)
  fmap f (Store key value next) = Store key value (f . next)

type LogicControl a = Free LogicControlMethod a

report :: Message -> LogicControl ()
report msg = Free (Report msg Pure)

store :: Key -> Value -> LogicControl ()
store key value = Free (Store key value Pure)

Scripts feel good now. You read them normally if they were just imperative
procedures:
storeJohnDoe :: LogicControl ()
storeJohnDoe = do
  store "First Name" "John"
  store "Last Name" "Doe"
  report "John Doe is written into KV DB."

Yet, it’s a question of what to do with EvalHdl that we had in the previous
design. Logic control scripts are kind of global, they know about every other
scripting language. We’ve quite failed in making this dependency between
LogicControl and Hdl ergonomic. List-based Hdl scripts couldn’t return
what they produced, they had to pass it deeper into LogicControl scripts
nested recursively. The absence of the “returning” feature occurred to be
damaging for the design. Remember this construction?
data LogicControlMethod
  = EvalHdl (Hdl LogicControl)
This is no longer needed. The Hdl type remains parametrized, but this
parametrization has a very different nature. A Free monadic nature. With this
nature, Hdl result is a script returning result. Or something specific, when
seen in the concrete business logic scripts such as in listing 6.2. Can we just
put Hdl result into that EvalHdl constructor?
data LogicControlMethod next
  = EvalHdl (Hdl result)

Do not forget about a continuation field! Intuition should now hint that the
continuation is a function that wants that result from the Hdl script.
Otherwise, where this value should go?
data LogicControlMethod next
  = EvalHdl (Hdl result) (result -> next)

This is the idea: we enclose the script, its returning value, and the
continuation within the single EvalHdl method. This will work indeed, except
in Haskell some extra feature is required, namely, Generalized Algebraic
Data Types:
{-# LANGUAGE GADTs #-}

data LogicControlMethod next
  = forall result. EvalHdl (Hdl result) (result -> next)

The LogicControlMethod is no longer a regular ADT, it’s a bit extended
type-level feature now. I believe, in other languages, this can be done using
some other tricks. At least, I did this in PureScript, a Haskell-like language
that lacks GADTs and Type Families. What to say, I even managed to do this
in C++, a language that must be quite far from Haskell… As a matter of fact,
modern C++ developers know that C++ and Haskell are close relatives, and
learning Haskell improves you in C++ almost magically.

The forall keyword is what makes LogicControlMethod be GADT. The
result type variable can’t escape the EvalHdl method, it’s localized there,
and should be only accessed with the method itself. If we place this variable
into the type itself, it will get a different meaning:
data LogicControlMethod result next
  = EvalHdl (Hdl result) (result -> next)

type LogicControl result a = Free (LogicControlMethod result) a

No GADTs are now required, but look: the result type variable is one for
the whole ADT, and it propagates upper into LogicControl. This means that
whenever we call a LogicControl script, all the Hdl insertings are obligated
to return the same type result. This will not compile:
myScript :: LogicControl ()
myScript = do
  evalHdl createBoosters                     -- Hdl (Controller, Controller)
  evalHdl createBoostersTwice                -- Hdl ()

Also, this “global” type variable prevents having different return types if we
want to call Hdl and DeviceControl scripts from LogicControl. So, the
forall keyword allows each occurrence to have its own return types. The
complete definition is shown in listing 6.6, variables are shortened for saving
the space:

Listing 6.6 The Free monadic LogicControl type

{-# LANGUAGE GADTs #-}

data LogicControlMethod next
  = forall a. EvalHdl (Hdl a) (a -> next)
  | forall a. EvalDeviceControlMethod (DeviceControlMethod a) (a -> next)
  | Report Message (() -> next)
  | Store Key Value (() -> next)

If you’re interested, I made DeviceControlMethod be GADT as well. I’ve
been trying to create some extensible type for commands, but this didn’t end
well so far. So I just stopped with some design that is okay for the chapter,
but I’d think more about the task and create something better instead. For
now, the DeviceControlMethod contains two actions, but distinct from the
old design, this type is GADT:
{-# LANGUAGE GADTs #-}

data DeviceControlMethod a where

  GetStatus
    :: Controller
    -> DeviceControlMethod (Either HardwareFailure ControllerStatus)

  ReadSensor
    :: Controller
    -> ComponentIndex
    -> DeviceControlMethod (Either HardwareFailure Measurement)

Notice that the code has a slightly different syntax. A short explanation for
the curious readers would be this. Each of the value constructors (GetStatus,
ReadSensor) corresponds to some action. Both have some arguments, as
usual functions do. You can even see the same syntax with double colons and
right arrows. Both actions are happened to be returning results. In contrast to
the Free monad mechanism, they utilize GADT to describe the fact of
returning. Hence we have this:
-> DeviceControlMethod (Either HardwareFailure ControllerStatus)
-> DeviceControlMethod (Either HardwareFailure Measurement)

The GADT syntax requires this kind of ending for each value constructor.
And the type itself should be parametrized. Later, when pattern-matching, we
can rely on the compiler and be sure that if our action is ReadSensor, then the
return value must be Either HardwareFailure Measurement. Briefly, this:
interpretDeviceControlMethod :: DeviceControlMethod a -> IO a

-- must return (Either HardwareFailure ControllerStatus)
interpretDeviceControlMethod (GetStatus _)
  = pure (Right ControllerOk)

-- must return (Either HardwareFailure Measurement)
interpretDeviceControlMethod (ReadSensor _ _)
  = pure (Right (Measurement Pressure 111.0))

Interestingly, we don’t need an extra type. It was HdlMethod - a bunch of
actions - and it was Hdl, - a Free monadic type, - but now it’s only
DeviceControlMethod, a GADT type, and it’s there included into
LogicControlMethod (as listing 6.5 shows). I would rather avoid using value
constructors directly though, because why not create convenient smart
constructors. There will be two sets of them: “low-level” and “high-level”.
“Low-level” mean they wrap data into DeviceControlMethod:
getStatus' :: Controller -> DeviceControlMethod (Either HardwareFailure Cont
getStatus' controller = GetStatus controller

readSensor' :: Controller -> ComponentIndex -> DeviceControlMethod (Either H
readSensor' controller idx = ReadSensor controller idx

They can’t be called monadically because DeviceControlMethod isn’t any
monad. However “high-level” functions wrap “low-level” once into the
LogicControl monad, so they are now full citizens of this world:

import qualified Andromeda.Hardware.Language.DeviceControl as DC

-- helper smart constructor:
evalDeviceControl :: DC.DeviceControlMethod a -> LogicControl a
evalDeviceControl dc = Free (EvalDeviceControlMethod dc Pure)

-- monadic functions for working with the device control subsystem:

getStatus :: Controller -> LogicControl (Either HardwareFailure ControllerSt
getStatus ctrl = evalDeviceControl (DC.getStatus' ctrl)

readSensor :: Controller -> ComponentIndex -> LogicControl (Either HardwareF
readSensor ctrl idx = evalDeviceControl (DC.readSensor' ctrl idx)

Just look what scripts we’re able to write now (listing 6.7)! This one creates
two booster devices and immediately requests for their statuses. The status
request procedures may fail as their types indicate, so we have to verify the
results via the pattern-matching:

Listing 6.7 Sample LogicControl script

import qualified Andromeda.LogicControl.Language as L

data LogicFailure = LogicFailure String

getBoostersStatus :: LogicControl (Either LogicFailure (ControllerStatus, Co
getBoostersStatus = do
  (lCtrl, rCtrl) <- L.evalHdl createBoosters
  eLStatus <- L.getStatus ctrl lCtrl
  eRStatus <- L.getStatus ctrl rCtrl
  let res = case (eLStatus, eRStatus) of
       (Right s1, Right s2) -> Right (s1, s2)
       (Left e, _) -> Left (LogicFailure (show e))
       (_, Left e) -> Left (LogicFailure (show e))
pure res

This is not an easy topic though, and definitely not the first time we’re
stepping on the advanced programming in Haskell. We probably should keep
control and stay on the subject of domain modeling while moving the talk on
types deeper in the book. Don’t worry if something isn’t clear enough, I just
hope that I succeeded with explaining the overall idea of having domain-
specific languages with Free monads.

The idea of impure stateful and even concurrent interpreters of those Free
monadic languages is yet to be explained in the next chapter though.

6.3.2 Advantages and disadvantages of a free language
What else should we know about the Free monad pattern? The following list
summarizes the pattern’s advantageous properties:

     Monadic. A monadic interface to an embedded language is much more
     convenient than just a functional one, especially for sequential and
     mutually dependent actions. Besides, you gain the power of standard
     monadic libraries, the functions of which will considerably enhance
     your monadic language.
     Readable and clean. A language that has a Free monad interface is
     usually self-descriptive. Monadic actions that the language is turned to
     will be obvious in usage.
     Safely composable and type-safe. Two monadic actions can be
     monadically composed; they have the same Free monad type. The base
     bricks of this monadic language are defined by your free language. This
     will add more static checking of the correctness of your free scripts.
     Abstract. A Free monad interface abstracts the underlying language.
     Scripts don't depend on the implementation details. It's even possible to
     completely replace the implementation of a language: all code contracts
     and invariants will be saved. The Free monad mechanism is an analog of
     the OOP interface in this sense.
     Interpretable. The Free monad pattern provides an easy way to interpret
     declarative definitions of domain entities into evaluable actions or into
     another language. Different interpreters serve different needs. For
     example, we’ll have interpreters: for tests and for real production; for
    concurrent runtime and for sequential-only runtime; for connecting to
    different external subsystems such as SQL DBs while having the same
    interface, and so on.
    Small adopting cost. To “free monadize” your language, which is
    probably an ADT, you make your language a functor and wrap it in the
    Free type. Unless you examine what's inside a Free monad library, it
    stays simple.
    Small maintaining cost. Modifying both a language and a monadic
    interface isn’t a big deal. You also need to keep the interpreters
    consistent, which can break some external code if the monadic interface
    isn’t stable, but this is a common problem of interfaces regardless of
    whether they are object-oriented or functional.

As you can see, the Free monad pattern has a lot of advantages over the
algebraic interfaces we were creating earlier. But there are some
disadvantages too:

    Because it's a monad, the user should know monads well to use your
    language.
    The Free monad requires that the language be a functor. Although it's an
    easy transformation for your language, you need to understand what it’s
    for. However, other Free monads such as Church-encoded Free monads,
    do not have this restriction. But possibly have their own, so the topic is a
    bit wider than described in this introductory chapter.
    The theory behind the Free monad is complex. For example, the Free
    type has a recursive value constructor that’s kind of the fixpoint notion
    (the mathematical concept of recursive operation). Also, converting a
    language into the Free monad form looks like a magical hack. But it's
    not magic, it's science. The good thing is that you can use the Free
