    monad in practice without knowing the theory at all.
    It's hard (sometimes impossible) to convert your language into the Free
    monad form when it’s made of advanced functional concepts: GADTs,
    phantom types, type-level logic, and so on. This probably means you
    need another approach to the problem.
    Some implementations of the Free monad can be slow as they
    approximate O(n^2) in the binding mechanism. But there are other Free
    monads, so this is not a problem.
At this point, you should be comfortable with simple and not even eDSLs. In
fact, there is much more to learn about Free monadic architectures. I would
say much, much more, - just evaluate the thickness of the rest of the book.
But don’t worry, it’s not about Free monads per se. The Functional
Declarative Design methodology has Free monads at its core, but the main
subject of it is the engineering approach. So we’ll be learning a lot of
engineering ideas in the next chapters, and we’ll see many different solutions,
not only Free monads.

Keep learning!

6.4 Summary
     Investigate the domain and define its properties, components, and laws.
     Based on the domain properties, design a set of domain languages in a
     form that’s more suitable and natural for expressing user scenarios.
     If your domain is supposed to have some imperative-like operations, or
     if you’d like to design your domain in a such way for some reason,
     consider wrapping your eDSLs into Free monads.
7 Stateful applications
This chapter covers
     Organizing stateful functional applications
     Creating pure and impure states
     Using state monads

What puzzles me from time to time is that I meet people who argue against
functional programming by saying that it can't work for real tasks because it
lacks mutable variables. No mutable variables, and therefore no state could
change, so interaction with the program isn’t possible. You might even be
hearing that “a functional program is really a math formula without effects,
and consequently, it doesn't work with memory, network, standard input and
output, and whatever else the impure world has. But when it does, it's not
functional programming anymore.” There are even more emotional opinions
and questions there, for example: “Is it cheating in Haskell when IO monad
just masks the imperative paradigm?” An impure code that Haskell's IO
monad abstracts over makes someone skeptical about how it can be
functional while it's imperative.

Functional programming doesn't imply the absence of any kind of state. It's
friendly to side effects but not so much as to allow them to vandalize our
code.

Here, we hit an important topic from the design standpoint. State in
functional programs:is it something special? In what manner should we
develop state and yet maintain the benefits of immutability? Are there any
best practices for organizing stateful code? I want to give you a
comprehensive answer to these questions. While being quite basic, the
discussion about state leads to really deep consequences in Software Design
that we’ll discover in the next chapters.

7.1 Stateful functional programming
Like most things in programming, a closer zoom into a topic reveals a huge
world of interesting structures. State isn’t an exclusion here. It’s not just
about values of data. State is a design category while values of data is a
coding category. What characterizes a design category you ask?

The first criterion we consider is lifetime.

     Local or auxiliary state. State that exists during a single calculation.
     This kind of state isn’t visible from the outside. The state variable will
     be created at the beginning and destroyed at the end of the calculation
     (note that with garbage collection, this may be true in a conceptual
     sense, but isn’t how it really is). The variable can freely mutate without
     breaking the purity of the code until the mutation is strictly
     deterministic.
     Operational state. State with a lifetime comparable to that of the
     application. This kind of state is used to drive the application’s business
     logic and to keep important user-defined data.
     External state. State with a lifetime exceeding that of the application.
     This state lives in external storage (databases) that provides long-term
     data processing.

The second division concerns a purity question. State can be

     Pure. Pure state isn’t really some mutable imperative variable that’s
     bound to a particular memory cell. Rather, it’s a functional imitation of
     mutability. We can also say that pure state doesn't destroy the previous
     value when assigning a new one. Pure state is always bounded by some
     pure calculation. In Haskell, there are two forms of pure state: argument-
     passing state and the State monad.
     Impure. Impure state always operates by dealing with impure side
     effects such as writing memory, files, databases, or imperative mutable
     variables. While an impure state is much more dangerous than a pure
     one, there are techniques that help to secure impure stateful calculations.
     Functional code that works with impure state can still be deterministic in
     its behavior. In Haskell, there is the variety of impure mutable variables:
     IORef, MVar, STM primitives such as TVar.

We learned from imperative programming the habit of handling mutable
variables like they are the most fundamental primitives possible. Teaching
how to deal with variables happens in the first lecture for every programming
language. But in functional languages, mutability never was a welcomed
guest. Lambda calculus and all the combinator calculi (like SKI) prove to us
that the essence of state isn’t in mutability of a distinct cell of memory. There
is no such category in those Turing complete systems. However, in practice,
we’d like to step away from purism and allow a real, destructive mutability
even in functional programs. Avoiding the impure state is possible although
the price will be a blatant wordy code. Not saying that sometimes mutable
imperative variables can possess a better performance compared to non-
mutable state transformations.

7.1.1 Simple stateful application
Remember the example with sandwiches from the previous chapter? We’ll
convert it into a finished stateful application and learn a specific architectural
pattern from it. We have everything for that; speaking figuratively, we’ve
collected all the ingredients for this sandwich application. Check out this
figure 7.1:

Figure 7.1 Simple stateful application
The interpreter has two inputs: script and ingredients. During the process,
some ingredients should be consumed and others should stay untouched. The
picture doesn’t tell us how the changes are handled by the interpreter. What
kind of state is this? We’re free to choose. It can be an impure state in form of
IORefs (as we’ve outlined in section 6.2.4). It also can be a pure immutable
argument-passing state that is so common in functional programs.

The simplest stateful application that makes a sandwich and prints the result
will have the main function to start from and some data to operate with. Let’s
bring all the pieces together. See listing 7.1 and notice that it makes a call to
the interpretRecipe interpreter:

Listing 7.1 Simple stateful application

-- Business logic
mySandwich :: SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich = do
  body1 <- startNewSandwich Toast Tomato
  body2 <- addComponent Cheese body1
  body3 <- addComponent Salt body2
  finishSandwich Nothing body3

-- Operational data
ingredients :: Ingredients
ingredients = Map.fromList
  [ (Tomato, 10), (Bread Toast, 6)
  , (Salt, 4), (Cheese, 5) ]

-- Application
main :: IO ()
main = do
  -- sudo make me a sandwich
  ingredientsRef <- newIORef ingredients
  sandwich <- interpretRecipe ingredientsRef mySandwich
  print sandwich

Don’t devalue program though, it does a good job in demonstrating different
layers. mySandwich is our business logic. It’s a Free monadic script that is
only aware of the interfaces in form of Free eDSLs. The interpretRecipe
interpreter is a sink into the implementation layer. Operational state is
represented by the ingredientsRef variable. All the layers have definite
boundaries, they play definite roles and are not allowed to interfere with other
layers. But they can’t just coexist, there should be a place like this main
function where everything is tied together. We call this specific place the
application layer.

Figure 7.2 demonstrates these primary layers and makes the pun about
sandwich application even funnier because the Free monadic architecture is
essentially a sandwich itself:

Figure 7.2 Layered Free monadic architecture
There are certainly other layers like the persistence layer, interoperability
layer, view layer, and so forth, - you can refresh your memories about layers
from chapter 2. Some of them have an application-wide significance while
others occur episodically. In the Haskell community, different approaches to
combining the layers exist, one is better than another. Choosing between
them is an architectural decision although it’s not always clear what will be
the long-term consequences. This is why it’s important to have some
fundamental principles such as KISS and SOLID which are helping us to
evaluate the decisions and satisfy the requirements better.

7.1.2 Purity, layering, state, and design

Business logic, interfaces, and implementation layers are no doubt the central
ones in such an architecture. However, as we know from the previous
chapters, there are two unavoidable, essential, intrinsic layers that exist in
every application. Those ones are pure and impure layers. It’s a modern idea
in Software Design to separate them explicitly, for example, with the IO
environment in Haskell, but it wasn’t so always. Impurity wasn’t a topic of
Software Design, and it was normal to just write impure code without
realizing this fact. If you like, impurity is the prose of Software Development
while purity is poetry.

Given this, state can be considered with respect to those layers, there is a
definite dependency here. Table 7.1 describes the relation between state and
purity layers:

Table 7.1 Purity layers and state



                                    Pure state    Impure state


Pure layer                          Allowed       Disallowed


Impure layer                        Allowed       Allowed


What does it mean in the context of architecture? Look at What does it mean
in the context of architecture? Look at figure 7.3 in which the arrows mean
“uses” or “knows about”:

Figure 7.3 Pure and impure layers
Here, both functions mySandwich and interpterRecipe can work with state,
but only interpretRecipe is allowed to be impure if you need so. The only
possibility of being pure for Free monadic scripts leads to a specific
consequence of the whole design: none of the implementation details are
welcomed on the left-up side of this diagram. And suddenly everything
becomes well-organized.

Once you start following this pattern, you realize that developing becomes
simpler and more conscious. It’s no longer blind hacking, it’s engineering.
Your reasoning changes from “how to shove some code into an unstructured
project and what is the most appropriate place for it” to “what is the interface
of this thing? should it be a subsystem with its own eDSL representation, or it
can be just a domain part enough satisfied with the current eDSL
capabilities?”. In other words, your attention to the business part becomes
less suppressed by unrelated matters, and you have more time to solve real
problems instead of artificially created ones.

For example, logging.

Logging is very needed in our programs, it’s the primary tool for collecting
debug info from the working application. Logging itself can be an
intimidating and difficult subject worth being discussed in own books. I’m
sure you know. Sigh.

Why not add some logging into our primitive sandwiches program and see
what’s happened. Do you think we can hammer down some logging calls into
the script itself?
-- external library
import Logger (LoggerConfig, LvlInfo, logMessage)

-- our wrapper
logInfo :: LoggerConfig -> String -> IO ()
logInfo cfg msg = logMessage LvlInfo cfg msg

-- script
mySandwich :: LoggerConfig -> SandwichRecipe Sandwich
mySandwich loggerCfg = do
  body1 <- startNewSandwich Toast Tomato

  logInfo loggerCfg "Sandwich started! Will be ready soon."

  body2 <- addComponent Cheese body1
  body3 <- addComponent Salt body2
  finishSandwich Nothing body3

This will not compile, even. Those logging functions are impure, the Free
monad SandwichRecipe just won’t allow those invocations. Also, passing
LoggerConfig into mySandwich feels so weird.

Do you think our SandwichConstructor eDSL will be happy to get logging
possibilities?
data SandwichConstructor next
  = StartNewSandwich BreadType Component (SandwichBody -> next)
  | AddComponent Component SandwichBody (SandwichBody -> next)
  | FinishSandwich (Maybe BreadType) SandwichBody (Sandwich -> next)
  | LogInfo Message (() -> next)

Definitely not: it doesn’t fit all other methods which are about the domain,
not about general domain-agnostic subsystem. It screams “why I’m here”.
This feeling is actually a design smell. We just committed a sin of Software
Design. We mixed different levels of abstraction and thus violated the
“divide and conquer” general principle.

So the only place that we can tell is left for logging could be the interpreter,
the impure one. I see some value in logging the errors before throwing
exceptions from their depths, namely from the withdrawIngredient
function. I could also log every step of sandwich creation when interpreting
the script. If that makes sense then okay, but I doubt it. Nevertheless, for the
sake of education, I’ll present you a part of such an interpreter in listing 7.2:

Listing 7.2 Impure interpreter with logging

withdrawIngredient :: IORef Ingredients -> Component -> IO ()
withdrawIngredient ingredsRef component = do
  ingreds <- readIORef ingredsRef
  case Map.lookup component ingreds of
    Just count | count > 0 -> do
      let ingreds' = Map.insert component (count - 1) ingreds
      writeIORef ingredsRef ingreds'
    _ -> do
      logError ("No ingredient: " <> show component)
      error ("No ingredient: " <> show component)

interpretStep :: IORef Ingredients -> SandwichConstructor a -> IO a
interpretStep ingredsRef (StartNewSandwich bread component next) = do
  logInfo ("Starting new sandwich: "
    <> show bread <> " "
    <> show component)
  withdrawIngredient ingredsRef component
  withdrawIngredient ingredsRef (Bread bread)
  let body = SandwichBody bread [component]
  pure (next body)

This is how we do a hidden, occasional logging solely on the implementation
layer. The business logic stays unaware of that, but every step is being
protocoled anyway.

You can reasonably say that we usually do need logging in to more
complicated domains. You’re completely right. We’ll be working on the
more real-world tasks in the rest of the books. We’ll discover ways to
produce interfaces for such subsystems. We’ll introduce the appropriate
eDSL design, and it won’t be violating those principles. It also won’t be
sacrificing purity and abstraction, and the beautiful scheme from diagram 7.3
will not be distorted.

7.2 State handling approaches
That was the reasoning behind making decisions about state and layering.
Now I believe it makes sense to briefly discuss state approaches and examine
concrete samples. Not the sandwich application though. It’s too simple and
barely representative. We’re returning to our Andromeda software and will
develop several different scripts and interpreters, all of them having their own
state handling mechanisms.

7.2.1 Argument-passing state
Do you remember the main idea of the LogicControl eDSL and its
components? Let me remind you: it’s the main programming environment to
write spaceship control scripts including calculations on how to move the
ship in space. Or, if you like, what engines should be enabled on what power
for what amount of time to achieve what momentum along what axis. This
everything requires a certain amount of math and physics to be involved.
Those calculations may be quite unobvious and difficult, they depend on
thousands of spaceship characteristics such as mass and the properties of the
components.

The following script is an example of what a piece of code for angular
impulse calculation could look like. The sample may be incorrect in this
regard, please forgive me for my poor knowledge of the physics of
spaceships, I only want to discuss the design.
getThrusterAngularImpulse
  :: Controller
  -> Mass
  -> LogicControl AngularImpulse
getThrusterAngularImpulse ctrl mass = do
  eMbTorqueProp <- getProperty ctrl "torque" []
  torque        <- validateTorque eMbTorqueProp
  calculateAngularImpulse mass torque

Do you want to know where those getProperty and validateTorque
functions came from? I’m definitely aware that some developers prefer to
control everything around them, and they can’t live without getting fluent in
every piece of code. Once they see a function, they search for the
implementation no matter that they could just catch a rough idea from the
name of the function and proceed. Possibly this is not the case with the
getThrusterAngularImpulse, so this is what’s about getProperty: this
function is a new member of the DeviceControl eDSL (and therefore is
callable from LogicControl) that allows you to request different properties of
devices in a generic yet loosely typed way.
getProperty
  :: Controller
  -> PropertyName
  -> [Param]
  -> LogicControl (Either LogicControlFailure (Maybe Property))

Maybe this function looks too generic, too untyped, but it’s here because it’s
nearly impossible to design a well-typed domain for all the peculiarities of
the various spaceship hardware. I’m almost sure there are attempts to build a
statically verifiable, massively type-level, and fully correctness-proven
SCADA software, but I barely believe those attempts are successful.

But that’s not my point, actually. It’s a talk about argument-passing state,
although, there is not that much to say.

We can think of Controller and Mass parameters as immutable state, and
they are arguments, obviously. However I honestly don’t think that
spaceships have a constant mass all the time. After all, they burn fuel and
emit combustion products into space. This is why we want to update the mass
when the ship gains or loses it. As an example, I’ll put here another script
performRotation that utilizes the previous one to calculate angular impulses,
recalculates mass, and performs engine two burns to actually rotate the ship.
In the end, the script returns an updated spaceship model with a new mass.
See listing 7.3:

Listing 7.3 Performing rotation script

data SpaceshipProperties = SpaceshipProperties
  { spMass :: Mass
  }

data SpaceshipModel = SpaceshipModel
  { smSpaceshipProperties :: SpaceshipProperties
  , smMainEngine          :: Controller
  , smRotationThruster    :: Controller
  }

performRotation
  :: SpaceshipModel
  -> Angle
  -> LogicControl SpaceshipModel
performRotation model angle = do
  let thrusterCtrl = smRotationThruster model
  let shipMass1    = spMass (smSpaceshipProperties model)

   impulse1 <- getThrusterAngularImpulse thrusterCtrl shipMass1
   shipMass2 <- recalcMass shipMass1 impulse1
   impulse2 <- getThrusterAngularImpulse thrusterCtrl shipMass2
   shipMass3 <- recalcMass shipMass2 impulse2

   (burnStart, burnStop, time) <- calcBurn impulse1 impulse2 angle

   performBurn thrusterCtrl burnStart
   wait time
   performBurn thrusterCtrl burnStop

   -- updating the state, returning new state
   pure (model { smSpaceshipProperties = SpaceshipProperties shipMass3 })

Again, no details on those intermediate steps are provided. Except maybe the
recalcMass, another function that uses the argument-passing state technique
for mass:
recalcMass :: Mass -> AngularImpulse -> LogicControl Mass
recalcMass shipMass impulse = ...
It’s always in this form:
get a value ->
do something with it ->
return an updated value

It doesn’t bring that much inconvenience compared to the impure mutable
state unless we deal with a sophisticated immutable data type consisting of
many separate components. If so, handling the immutable state becomes too
cumbersome.

For example, spaceship properties might contain not only mass but also
coordinates and orientation, current orbital speed, and many other
characteristics.
data SpaceshipProperties = SpaceshipProperties
  { spMass                     :: Mass
  , spOrbitalCoordinates       :: OrbitalCoordinates
  , spOrientation              :: Orientation
  , spOrbitalSpeed             :: Speed
  , spManyOtherCharacteristics :: ManyOtherCharachteristics
  }

Imagine the wordiness of updating constructions in the code!
func :: SpaceshipModel -> LogicControl SpaceshipModel
func model1 = do
  newProps1 <- doSmth model1
  let model2 = model1 {smSpaceshipProperties = newProps1}
  newProps2 <- doSmth model2
  let model3 = model2 {smSpaceshipProperties = newProps2}
  model4 <- doManyOtherThings model3
  pure model4

doSmth :: SpaceshipModel -> LogicControl SpaceshipProperties
doManyOtherThings :: SpaceshipModel -> LogicControl SpaceshipModel

I assumed that all those functions return bare values however the more
realistic codebase will have many methods incrusted with error handling
mechanisms such as the Either and Maybe types. The code will grow
tremendously in this case.
func :: SpaceshipModel -> LogicControl SpaceshipModel
func model1 = do
  eNewProps1 <- doSmth model1
  case eNewProps1 of
    Left err -> error err
    Right newProps1 -> ...

doSmth
  :: SpaceshipModel
  -> LogicControl (Either LogicControlFailure SpaceshipProperties)

Haskell developers don’t really like such a boilerplate, and they found ways
to do instead. Have you heard about the State monad? You can expect to
meet it in a regular Haskell codebase although other languages experience
less invasion of it because of the inherent mutability available to developers.
No doubt the State monad approach should be put on the table due to its
specific role in the design of our stateful programs.

7.2.2 Pure state with the State monad
Rockets can’t lift off from green grass and jump straight to the orbit. There
must be a reliable launchpad equipped with all the important subsystems: fuel
supply, crane system, and a solid launch silo. In the same way, the
introduction to State monads can’t start without understanding the origins of
monads, their history and the corresponding math, and what’s so special
about Category Theory here.

Oh, come on, how can I believe this sentence when I have the IO monad and
the Free monad widely represented on the previous pages. The introduction
into State monads can be just a small piece of code that demonstrates its
usage. No need to know what a monad is, or how the State monad is
constructed in order to use it. Did I make this point already? Nevertheless,
worth repeating it again.

The State monad is cunning. The code will be pretending that the state is
mutable while it isn’t. The state variable will be available to all your monadic
functions in the monadic do-environment so that you could either read or
update the state whenever you need to. Those reading and writing functions
(get and put) are all implemented for you already, you get them instantly
once you say that your function works within this monad.
import Control.Monad.State (State, get, put)
type Age = Int

gettingOlder :: State Age String
gettingOlder = do
  age <- get                    -- getting the Age variable
  let newAge = age + 1
  put newAge                    -- updating the Age variable
  pure ("You're now older for 1 year: " <> show newAge)

As you can see, the State monad comes with the type parameter for denoting
what state you want to maintain. The other type parameter denotes the return
type as it’s usual for all the monads:
someFunc :: State someState someOutput

Certainly, calling the identically monadized functions from each other means
that the state will be the one and only like there was a shared mutable
variable somewhere in the context of those functions.
bornAndGettingOlderTwice :: State Age [String]
bornAndGettingOlderTwice = do
  put 0                        -- born

  msg1 <- gettingOlder                -- Age == 1
  msg2 <- gettingOlder                -- Age == 2

  age   <- get                        -- should be 2 now

  pure [msg1, msg2, "Your age now: " <> show age]

After the actual running of this script you’re in your right to expect that two
sequential invocations of gettingOlder will change to 2 the Age state they
have in common.

I say “they have in common” while knowing that the internal mechanism
doesn’t actually do any state sharing, it doesn’t do any mutation, and there is
no the common context as it can seem from the script. In fact, the internal
mechanism operates as argument-passing state that is smartly wrapped into
the monad machinery. If you start digging deeper, you’ll know how the bind
((>>=)) operator makes it possible to pass the state from one monadic
function to another (see figure 7.4). It’s amazing that this behavior is
discovered to be monadic, and as a consequence, it becomes quite handy in
the do-notation form.

Figure 7.4 Schematic behavior of the State monad
Should I tell more about the implementation of State monads? I don’t think
so. We as users, can completely ignore it and enjoy the clearness of the code.
Knowing the details might be important in the production setting, for
example, from the performance point of view, but otherwise, it’s an
abstraction, and like any other abstraction, it can be only observed as is,
without opening this Pandora's box.

I avoid teaching you functional programming per se. I don’t teach lambdas,
continuations, or high-order functions. I even refuse to reveal the hidden
mechanisms of state monads or other concepts abundantly scattered here on
the pages. Those things are too low-level nuts and bolts and they are enough
explained in other materials. For those of you who need a lesson on monad
stacks and monad transformers, I definitely recommend the great Haskell
books by Will Kurt and Vitaly Bragilevsky.

LINK


Will Kurt, Get Programming With Haskell
https://www.manning.com/books/get-programming-with-haskell

LINK


Vitaly Bragilevsky, Haskell in Depth
https://www.manning.com/books/haskell-in-depth

Don’t forget about practice though. Programming is a practice in the first
place. If you feel lost in the material of my book, consider practicing more. I
don’t apologize, quite the opposite: I promise that the further we go, the more
concepts will occur.

Running of a State monadic script, however, should be demonstrated. Many
monads have this notion of running, including the State monad, the Free
monad, and, for example, the STM (Software Transactional Memory) monad.
For the State monad, it’s a no-brain notion, really. You just need to specify
the initial state (for example, Age variable set to 111), and the script itself.
The runState function will then consume those two arguments and produce
the final state among the value the script wants to return.
runState
  :: State state returnValue
  -> state
  -> (state, returnValue)

Consider the test that passes:
import Control.Monad.State (runState)

spec :: Spec
spec =
  describe "State monad tests" $ do
    it "Age script" $ do
      let (msgs, s) = runState bornAndGettingOlderTwice 111

       s `shouldBe` 2
       msgs `shouldBe` [ "You're now older for 1 year: 1"
                       , "You're now older for 1 year: 2"
                       , "Your age now: 2"
                       ]

Alright, cool, brilliant. Amazing. Exciting. Learning state monads with such
toy programs. Definitely the level of maturity we deserve!

I’m sure you’ll forgive me for my sarcasm because we both understand: if I
started talking about some concept, I have a plan to utilize it for our main
tasks. Greatly appreciated in code bases, the State monad has many faces and
forms some of which can be joined with other monads to achieve similar
state-like behavior. I’m certainly talking about the StateT monad transformer
that is able to wrap around any other monad and make its scripts purely
stateful. Our custom-crafted monads LogicControl and Hdl can be also
empowered by StateT. With almost no additional effort, we’ll get stateful
scripts having less boilerplate than it’s with argument-passing state. Ready to
talk about it?

7.2.3 Bringing pure state into your monadic eDSLs
From the developer's perspective, monad transformers such as StateT do the
same thing as their counterpart monads such as State. StateT brings a
pseudo-mutable pure state into monadic chains with the same functions to
deal with it: get and put. The difference is that apart from this StateT,
another monad of your choice will be fully available, with only a little bit of
ceremonies. Technically speaking, the StateT monad transformer wraps
another monad and thus forms a two-layer monadic stack.

TIP


I’m certainly concerned to help you in understanding, and this is why I wrote
two appendices about monad transformers: Appendix B: Layering subsystems
with monadic stack, Appendix: Word statistics example with monad
transformers. Other materials are also good for this.

Let me introduce you to a very common monadic stack with StateT on the
top and IO on the bottom. See figure 7.5.

Figure 7.5 StateT and IO monadic stack




I’m sure I’ve seen two or three monad tutorials with this stack helping people
in game development. For example, if we’d want to implement a
Minesweeper game with it, we could go with the following definitions:
import Control.Monad.State (StateT)

data CellType
  = Bomb
  | Num Int
  | Empty

data Cell
  = Hidden CellType
  | Revealed CellType
type Board = Map (X, Y) Cell

type BoardMonad a = StateT Board IO a

This BoardMonad will have some state (a value of Board) and also will be able
to do any IO call. To reveal a cell and update the state of this cell we could
write a script that asks the board from the context, checks the cell, updates the
cell, and puts the new board back into the context. During this process, we
may print something about the cell to the console with an impure invocation
of putStrLn.
import Control.Monad.State (get, put, lift)

revealCell :: (X, Y) -> BoardMonad ()
revealCell (x, y) = do
  board <- get

  lift (putStrLn ("Trying cell " <> show (x, y)))

  case Map.lookup (x, y) board of
    Nothing -> lift (putStrLn "No such cell.")
    (Just (Revealed _)) -> lift (putStrLn "Already revealed.")
    (Just (Hidden Bomb)) -> lift (putStrLn "It’s bomb! You loose!")
    (Just (Hidden c)) -> do
      let newBoard = Map.insert (x, y) (Revealed c) board
      put newBoard
      lift (putStrLn "Cell revealed.")

Two things to notice in this script. First, StateT is a monad itself, so the do-
notation works as usual. Second, we have to wrap IO calls with the lift
function because the monad of the do block is StateT, not IO. The lift
function, therefore, has a role to be an adapter between monads in the stack:
lift :: IO a -> StateT Board IO a

-- the same:
lift :: IO a -> BoardMonad a

Or course, other monad stacks will have their own lift function. Or even if
you decide to build a longer monad stack, you’ll be doing more liftings in a
row. The following strange but valid data type will require one or two lifts
depending on what exactly layer you want to interact with:
type Score = Int
type MinesweeperGame a = StateT Score BoardMonad a

printGame :: MinesweeperGame ()
printGame = do

  score <- get                               -- no lifting
  board <- lift get                          -- lift once

  lift (lift (putStrLn (                     -- lift twice
    "Score: " <> show score
    <> ", board: " <> show board
    )))

What about running, there is a specific runStateT function for this. Guess
how many of them should be called when there are two StateT transformers
in the stack? That’s right: one for each, two in total. This is a bit tricky
though because running unwraps the monadic stack inside out. And I’d rather
go with execStateT instead. It will return the final state, and won’t return the
boring result that is always () for printGame. These two will have the
following types when applied:
execStateT :: MinesweeperGame a -> Score -> BoardMonad Score
execStateT :: BoardMonad a -> Board -> IO Board

To finally demonstrate the calls, I wrote two tests that pass:
board1 :: Board
board1 = Map.fromList
  [ ( (1, 1), Hidden Empty )
  , ( (2, 2), Hidden Bomb )
  ]

board2 :: Board
board2 = Map.fromList
  [ ( (1, 1), Revealed Empty )
  , ( (2, 2), Hidden Bomb )
  ]

spec :: Spec
spec =
  describe "StateT monad tests" $ do
    it "BoardMonad stack" $ do
      (_, board') <- runStateT (revealCell (1, 1)) board1
      board' `shouldBe` board2
     it "MinesweeperGame stack" $ do
       let runGame :: BoardMonad Score = execStateT printGame 0
       let runBoard :: IO Board = execStateT runGame board1

        board' <- runBoard
        board' `shouldBe` board1

Picture 7.6 illustrates the corresponding monadic stack:

Figure 7.6 Three layer monadic stack




NOTE


For some Haskell-specific reason the BoardMonad data type is defined a bit
differently in the sources:
type BoardMonad = StateT Board IO

instead of
type BoardMonad a = StateT Board IO a

This shrinking of the return type parameter seems strange, but due to
limitations of the Haskell’s type system, this is the only form that will allow
us to define the MinesweeperGame data type:
type MinesweeperGame a = StateT Score BoardMonad a

This topic is irrelevant to us, but hope you won’t be surprised when playing
with the code.

Now, after those preparations, it should not be difficult to construct a stateful
LogicControl scenario and remove that ugly boilerplate. No matter that our
LogicControl is a Free eDSL, it’s a monad and therefore will finely work
with StateT.

With this tool, the code about spaceship rotation from listing 7.3 can be
improved, see listing 7.4. I also think that those functions were designed
badly in many senses, so I reworked them a little. In particular, calculation
functions should be pure because we don’t expect them to change the
properties of the ship. On the other hand, performBurn should affect mass, so
this function can stay monadic in the new monad ShipControl. Certainly,
there are no obstacles for calling LogicControl functions from ShipControl
scripts, you just need some little lifting as I did with getProperty and
validateTorque functions.

Listing 7.4 Stateful script for performing rotation

import Control.Monad.State (StateT, put, get, lift)

-- Stateful monadic type
type ShipControl a = StateT SpaceshipModel LogicControl a

-- Stateful script
performRotation :: Angle -> ShipControl ()
performRotation angle = do

   model :: SpaceshipModel <- get

   let thrusterCtrl = smRotationThruster model
   let shipMass1 = spMass $ smSpaceshipProperties model

   eMbTorqueProp <- lift (getProperty thrusterCtrl "torque" [])
   torque        <- lift (validateTorque eMbTorqueProp)

   let impulse1 = calcAngularImpulse shipMass1 torque
   let shipMass2 = recalcMass shipMass1 impulse1
   let impulse2 = calcAngularImpulse shipMass1 torque
  let shipMass3 = recalcMass shipMass2 impulse2
  let (burnStart, burnStop, time) = calcBurn impulse1 impulse2 angle

  performBurn thrusterCtrl burnStart                        -- mass changes
  lift $ wait time
  performBurn thrusterCtrl burnStop                         -- mass changes

Interesting that not only LogicControl scripts can be called with lifting. Vice
versa: invoke ShipControl functions from LogicControl scripts with the
same mechanism of running the state. You should be able to construct a
SpaceshipModel value though:

import Control.Monad.State (execStateT)

spaceshipRotation :: LogicControl SpaceshipModel
spaceshipRotation = do

  mainEngineCtrl <- L.evalHdl Test.createMainEngine
  thrusterCtrl   <- L.evalHdl Test.createRotaryThruster

  let shipProps = SpaceshipProperties (Kilogram 100000.0)
  let shipModel1 = SpaceshipModel shipProps mainEngineCtrl thrusterCtrl

  shipModel2 <- execStateT (performRotation (Radian 100.0)) shipModel1

  pure shipModel2

In this case, state becomes localized, defined for only the part of business
logic. It’s only a virtual state, nothing actually mutates during its evaluation.
You could achieve the same with argument passing, but sometimes
introducing StateT makes your scripts more convenient and concise. We
transformed our business logic into a stateful application without adding new
features, without sacrificing purity, and without bringing that much
complexity. Really good stuff, congratulations!

NOTE


I wish I could say that wrapping LogicControl into StateT doesn’t even
affect the framework we’ve built. Unfortunately, this is untrue. This won’t
compile in Haskell:
type LogicControl a = Free LogicControlMethod a
type ShipControl a = StateT SpaceshipModel LogicControl a

> Compilation error: The type synonym ‘LogicControl’ should have 1 argument,

Due to the deep reasons, we have to convert LogicControl to a newtype,
define all the instances of Functor, Applicative and Monad we had
previously from the Free monadic type. After that, we’ll be able to put
LogicControl into StateT. If you’re not familiar with those concepts, it’s
okay, I’ll just show you how to derive the instances with the
GeneralizedNewtypeDeriving feature:

{-# LANGUAGE GeneralizedNewtypeDeriving #-}
newtype LogicControl a = LogicControl (Free LogicControlMethod a)
  deriving (Functor, Applicative, Monad)
type ShipControl a = StateT SpaceshipModel LogicControl a

Fortunately, not a single word of business logic code is affected by that. We
have to fix interpreters though. Some unwrapping will be needed for this
newtype, hopefully not a big deal:
runLogicControl :: LogicControl a -> IO a
runLogicControl (LogicControl lControl) = ...

I’m certainly not giving you all the explanations of this issue, just because it
will lead us into the depths of Haskell. A curious reader may want to read
materials on partial type synonyms and undecidability.

7.2.4 Impure mutable state

While our business logic should stay pure, the implementation behind the
eDLSs is free from such limitations. Interpreters are impure by default hence
are capable to deal with mutable values and collections. Of course, you could
keep some of your Free interpreters pure if that makes sense for a specific
language, yet I’d expect them all to be in the IO environment.

This would make even more sense if we bring to the table the question of
performance. Sometimes you can be pleasured with pure persistent data
structures that are efficient enough for many cases. However C-like arrays
which are guaranteed to be continuous, fast, and efficient, are also impure
and destructively mutable. Impure mutable data structures can do all the stuff
we like in imperative languages, are less demanding to memory, can be
mutated in place, and can even be marshaled to low-level code in C. This is
what your interpreters may do behind the interpretable eDSLs, and I find it
very similar to what OOP interfaces and implementation classes do.

Scala, Haskell, F#, PureScript - all of these languages have their own tools
for that. There is some intersection between them though. You may or may
not find the following Haskell things in your language, but I’m sure it’s
worth learning anyway:

     Haskell's IORef variable has exactly the same semantics as a regular
     variable in other languages. It can be mutated in place, making potential
     problems (nondeterminism, race conditions, and so on) the developer's
     responsibility. The IORef a type represents a reference type[1] over
     some type a. In Scala, there are various mutable references from various
     libraries, not saying about the actual mutability Scala provides.
     MVar is a concept of thread-safe mutable variables. Unlike the IORef,
     this reference type gives guarantees of atomic reading and writing. MVar
     can be used for communication between threads or managing simple use
     cases with data structures. Still, it's susceptible to the same problems:
     race conditions, deadlocks, and nondeterminism. Scala also has MVars
     with a similar interface.
     TVar, TMVar, TQueue, TArray, and other STM primitives can be thought
     of as further development of the MVar concept. STM primitives are
     thread-safe and imperatively mutable, but unlike MVar, STM introduces
     transactions. Every mutation is performed in a transaction. When two
     threads compete for access to the variable, one of two transactions will
     be performed, while the other can safely delay (be retried) or even
     rollback. STM operations are isolated from each other, which reduces
     the possibility of deadlock. With the advanced combinatorial
     implementation of STM, two separate transactional operations can be
     combined into a bigger transactional operation that’s also an STM
     combinator. STM is considered a suitable approach for maintaining
     complex state in Haskell programs; with that said, STM has many
     characteristics and properties one should know about to use it
     effectively.
Earlier in chapters 4, 5, and 6, we’ve already introduced IORefs in the code.
It wasn’t a thorough discussion though, I just demonstrated a couple of
design options and promised to talk about it later. In Chapter 4, we’ve been
talking about services and the Service Handle pattern. We found a way to
mock services which in turn helped us write unit tests. A mocked service
could report its internal state to the outside by writing into the corresponding
IORef variable. There was the Hardware service and some counter that counts
how many invocations of getDevicePart are done:
mockedHardwareService :: IORef Int -> HardwareService
mockedHardwareService counterRef = HardwareService
  { getDevicePart = mockedGetDevicePart counterRef
  }

mockedGetDevicePart
  :: IORef Int
  -> ComponentIndex
  -> Device
  -> IO (Maybe DevicePart)
mockedGetDevicePart counterRef idx device = do
  modifyIORef' counterRef (+1)
  ...

In Chapter 5, the interpreters of the various eDSLs were done stateful. All we
needed for that was a specific, implementation-layer-only data structure
called Runtime. This structure contains everything for the interpreters to
operate, including the internal state, temporary values, possibly configs,
database or network connections, thread pools, and threads themselves. This
all should be hidden from the business logic, but at the same time, no one
application can work without this mutable, operational data.
data Runtime = Runtime
  { _devicesRef         :: IORef Devices
  , _hardwareServiceRef :: IORef HardwareService
  }

interpretLogicControlMethod :: Runtime -> LogicControlMethod                -> IO ()

runLogicControl :: Runtime -> LogicControl -> IO ()

I can easily imagine an interpretable eDSL for a Turing-complete
programming language that will be doing its business with the runtime. Let’s
say, it’s Brainfuck:
incPointer   = ">"
decPointer   = "<"
incVal       = "+"
decVal       = "-"
input        = ","
output       = "."
jumpForward = "["
jumpBackward = "]"

type Cell = Integer
type Val = Integer

data Runtime = Runtime
  { pointer :: IORef Cell
  , fields :: IORef (Map Cell (IORef Val))
  }

type BFProgram = String

interpretBF
  :: Runtime                            -- runtime
  -> BFProgram                          -- script
  -> IO ()
interpretBF rt (ch:chs) = ...           -- do smth with Runtime

helloWorld :: String
helloWorld =
  "++++++++[>++++[>++>+++>+++>+<<<<-]>+>+>->>
   +[<]<-]>>.>---.+++++++..+++.>>.<-.<.+++.--
   ----.--------.>>+.>++."

It feels so natural to implement programming languages this way! Every
compiler you take will have this separation of states: one for “business logic”
- the actual programming code, another one for “runtime” - literally, the
runtime of a compiler. This tells us that the idea of separating the two worlds
is fundamental, and reveals deep connections between programming
languages, DSLs, and business domains. Just compare the interpretBF
function with interpretRecipe one from Chapter 6!
interpretRecipe
  :: IORef Ingredients                   -- runtime
  -> SandwichRecipe Sandwich             -- script
  -> IO Sandwich
With that, real software today gets more complicated than our toy examples.
The only fact that we mostly deal with concurrency makes IORefs be not a
good tool to keep internal data. In such a concrete application as an HTTP
backend, a RESTful server should be able to respond to parallel, concurrent
requests and not mess things up in the operational state. This means, data in
runtimes should be designed thread-safety, with help of the corresponding
data structures (MVars or STM):
data Runtime = Runtime
  { _devicesRef         :: TVar Devices
  , _hardwareServiceRef :: TVar HardwareService
  }

Approaches to concurrency can be difficult and intimidating to a person
having a limited background in multithreading. We absolutely need this
knowledge in our day-to-day practice, but we don’t have to learn the whole
subject. Instead, we’ll discover very convenient and very nice approaches
such as concurrent MVars and STM, and will see that even this accidental
complexity can be nicely beaten and controlled.

7.3 Summary
     Functional programming doesn’t prohibit state. In opposite, it provides
     new ideas for building safe and carefully designed stateful applications.
     State can be pure and impure. Pure business logic may enjoy with pure
     state while implementation can deal with impure, mutable one.
     State monads can empower the way we structure our monadic
     applications.
[1] Reference type in Wikipedia:
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference_type
8 Reactive applications
This chapter covers
     What is actor model and how to design it
     Learning the MVar request-response pattern
     Creating a stateful concurrent simulator
     Learning more on reactive architectures

Looking back at the work we have done together we can be proud of
ourselves. We have learned a lot, we discussed many interesting ideas in
software design, and we tackled a variety of engineering problems. The
solutions and approaches we’ve explored are all inhabiting my methodology
Functional Declarative Design - a methodology for building applications
from end to end in functional languages. This chapter finishes the proof that
the methodology really works. We’re going to finally assemble many parts of
our Andromeda software and produce the final result of it - the simulator. I
promised we’ll do it, and here we are.

I’m sure this is a good time to talk about reactive applications. Our simulator
will be this: an application having different individual parts able to react to
something. “Individual” here means those parts will have their own threads,
and they will act as small separate applications. In other words, in this
chapter, we’ll slightly touch on a specific reactive model of interactions,
namely, the actor model.

This also means that we should talk about concurrency. And we will! Actors
can be implemented even if have a single thread, and they will have all the
properties of a concurrent system. You won’t tell this system from a truly
multithreaded one without additional investigations. Regardless, a single-
threaded concurrency is rarely a go-to for production codebases. Our actor
model will be classically multithreaded, with concurrent behavior and state.
And by the way, the knowledge about state handling obtained from the
previous chapter will find essential improvement.
8.1 Actor model
Most of the physical characteristics of the flight are dynamic and time-
dependant. The mass of a ship changes while the engines are burning and fuel
is being consumed. Coordinates of the ship change in relation to pivotal
objects such as stars, planets. The ship may have rotation along some axis,
and this is also a varying characteristics to consider.

The math of this process isn’t simple, it’s differential equations all down the
road. Given some inputs, a formula will expectedly return a single result. For
example, there is a Tsiolkovsky's formula for rocket-like motion that is
needed to calculate masses of fuel and the ship so that it was possible to
launch it into the orbit. For a vessel in space, there are many formulas which
are describing all the dynamic life of all the interconnected systems. Having a
good equation for motion means having an efficient, safe flight. But it’s
barely possible to have everything precalculated before it. Survival of an
expedition often depends on how well those formulas react to new data
coming from around the environment. The same formulas should be then
alive and should be fed by data to produce new results constantly.

This all sounds like a good task for reactive programming. Actors, reactive
streams, custom-crafted event-driven models, or other exotic reactive models
- all of these approaches may help to organize the environment for your
spaceship’s formulas to live in. And we should learn some of them to explore
this area of Software Design.

8.1.1 Simulation of actors

With some period of sampling, sensors can produce measurements and push
those measurements to whatever consumer wants to consume. Controllers
may sit on top of the data flow and evaluate calculations on the fly, thus
making decisions on what else commands should be propagated to the
devices. The navigation system can do small corrections to the route
according to the program a central computer has established. All the systems
and devices act like musicians in a symphony of a spaceship flight.

Conceptually, devices can be seen as actors, all of them having a state,
lifetime, public interface, and a specified behavior. Each device will then
represent a separate process, thread, or even machine. It doesn’t really matter
what exactly implementation is chosen for the actor model. To make it work,
there should also be a certain medium, an environment for the devices to
communicate through. Again, the options are various: message brokers, a
database, an event bus, or something else. This really depends on your high-
level requirements and the constraints you have to comply with. Two of the
options are visualized in figure 8.1. The left picture treats a network displaced
system as separate actors with the TCP/IP communication channel between
them. It’s maybe not a true message broker, but you can put Kafka between
the computers, and you’ll get it. The right picture already supposes that there
is a queue inbetween. Kafka, or maybe RabbitMQ or something like that.

Figure 8.1 Two actor model implementations




In our case, the environment will only be limited by a single application, the
simulator. The parts of a spaceship will act independently, being individual
green (lightweight) threads. The simulator will then be responsible for the
orchestration of their lifetimes and interactions, but it should not be visible to
them. All the actors should be unaware of the environment they are evaluated
in. The model of the ship becomes therefore a virtual reality, and we can
expect it to behave close to the real spaceship once we deploy the same
model into it. See figure 8.2 for more hints:

Figure 8.2 Real environment and virtual environment




As you can notice, we rely on an abstracted communication protocol that
hides the underlying world. The protocol described in terms of requests and
responses will be a contract, an interface, an agreement all the actors should
be comfortable with. So we can say the protocol is a part of our domain, and
even a glue for agents of our simulation model.

Obviously, we will be obtaining the simulation model from our logic control
scripts because what else could play this role for us. It will be exactly those
scripts we’ve been developing with the Domain-Driven Design approach,
nothing extra is needed, and no changes are expected. Embedded domain-
specific languages such as Hdl will therefore be appropriate for both worlds.
They are interfaces to real subsystems, so a spaceship engineer assumes they
program a real spaceship with them. At the same time, the scripts will define
all the properties of the simulated actors. We can call this transformation
from scripts to a simulator model a “compilation”. Figure 8.3 illustrates this:

Figure 8.3 Compilation from logic control scripts to specific environments
Again, it’s another proof that interpreting can do powerful things. This is
certainly a conceptual idea we’ll be implementing here. But prior to that, we
as should discuss specific technical details about the simulator.

8.1.2 The architecture of the simulator
In fact, the compilation of the simulation model is again about traversing our
interpretable eDSLs and scripts. We’ll write new interpreters for that, and
they will be quite the same structurally but certainly mind their own business.
New interpreters should not know about the “real” ones, should not interfere
with them anyhow, and in general, the code of the simulator can be (and
ideally should be) placed into a separate project or repository. We’ll keep it
simple, and put the simulator close to the “real” code. This is a selected list of
modules of the simulator:
./src/Andromeda/Simulator
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/Hardware/Interpreters/DeviceControl.hs
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/Hardware/Interpreters/Hdl.hs
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/Hardware/Device.hs
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/LogicControl/Interpreter.hs
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/Control.hs
./src/Andromeda/Simulator/Runtime.hs

This structure is already familiar to us. Free monadic architecture comes with
this best practice. We again see the Runtime module, the Device module, and
some interpreters. The uniformity of the simulator and the real code eases the
navigation across the projects. Following this best practice is strictly
recommended, although it might not be the “perfect” way to organize the
code. We don’t say other big frameworks such as Ruby On Rails are perfect,
but once you joined an RoR project, you can expect that your experience is
finally applicable. So “best” here means “pragmatic”, “time-saving” and
“well-spread”. We just help developers to take their effort towards important
things: business logic, edge cases, and tests, and do not spend much time
inventing new ways of doing things.

Let me demonstrate to you the architecture of the simulator in figure 8.4:

Figure 8.4 Architecture of the simulator
This is the same three-layered architecture, with just a new implementation
layer. Having three distinct implementations for the same interface (“real”,
for tests, for the simulator) proves that the Free monadic architecture does
indeed have similar power as OOP interfaces in other languages.

Now we have many architectural questions about the simulator to answer
before we can jump into the code. Let’s take a short questionnaire here then.

     What is an actor? Every device in the network having a controller will
     be an actor. All the communication will go through the controller, and
     there will be no way to directly access the internal components of a
     device.
     What actors are, technically? They are green threads with their own
     behavior, mutable state, and lifetime.
     Are the actors independent? In general, actors should be isolated and
     should not know about each other. However, we’ll implement a
     hierarchy of actors while following our logic control scripts. You can
     consult figure 5.3: it has a central computer that keeps tracking of the
     four subordinate controller devices.
     How do the actors exchange data and communicate? We’ll adopt a
     certain design pattern for that, it’s called MVar Request-Response
     pattern. Our environment is multithreaded, so we should do it carefully.
     This pattern makes it easy for several distinct threads to talk without
     race conditions and deadlocks. For this to happen, we’ll need to utilize
     concurrent mutable variables (MVars) in a certain way. We’ll learn this
     pattern in the next section. Also, we could also take STM (Software
     Transactional Memory) instead MVars. Being a better choice for this
     task, STM would be too much for this chapter to introduce. We’ll learn
     it in the next chapters though.
     How do the actors manage their internal state? When needed, actor
     workers will be keeping their data in MVars.
     Are the actors is another way to say “The truest Object-Oriented
     Programming by Alan Kay”?

What about the last question, the story behind shows how tricky can be paths
of the ideas which are moving through time and people’s consideration. I
highly recommend to expand your horizons and read more about OOP by
Alan Kay!

8.1.3 The MVar Request-Response pattern
The idea behind a concurrently mutable variable (or, for short, MVar) is
pretty simple. The variable of the MVar a type may hold one value of type a or
hold nothing. The mutation of MVar is a real mutation in the sense of
imperative programming, but what’s important is that it's thread-safe. Since
the notion of the MVar involves mutation, it works on the IO layer. The
MVar interface consists of the creation functions
newEmptyMVar :: IO (MVar a)
newMVar      :: a -> IO (MVar a)

and the blocking functions for reading and writing contents
takeMVar :: MVar a -> IO a
putMVar :: MVar a -> a -> IO ()

A value of type MVar a can be in two states: either empty or full. When it's
empty, every thread that calls takeMVar will wait until it's full. Conversely,
when the MVar is full, a thread that calls putMVar will wait until it's empty.
Concurrency means that threads can't access the MVar simultaneously; they
must wait their turn (first in, first out), and that's why the MVar can't have an
invalid state. But classic deadlocks are still possible. Each taking of an MVar
with takeMVar should be paired by putMVar sooner or later. A badly
organized branching in the code may lead to no putMVar called. While this
specific thread continues doing its job, the variable is yet owned, and other
threads will just hang forever.

TIP


MVars have other behavioral issues you might want to know about. To learn
more about these, consider reading “Parallel and Concurrent Programming in
Haskell” by Simon Marlow (O’Relly, 2013).

With MVars, it's possible to construct a remote service that works in a
separate thread, waits until a request is pushed, processes it, and pushes the
result back. This is known as the MVar request–response pattern. Let's take a
look at its structure.

The MVar request–response pattern operates with two MVars: one for the
request and one for the response. These MVars together represent a
communication channel that can abstract any kind of interaction between two
threads, namely a requester and a respondent. The basic idea and workflow
are shown in figure 8.5.

Figure 8.5 The MVar request–response pattern flow
The requester performs two steps:

     A. Put a value into the request MVar.
     B. Take the result from the response MVar.

If the responder isn't yet ready to accept the request, the A step will make the
requester wait. The B step will block the requester if the processor is busy
processing the previous request.

The responder does the opposite. It performs three steps:

     C. Take the request value from the request MVar.
     D. Process the request to get the response.
     E. Put the response into the response MVar.

If a request hasn't been placed yet, the C step will block the responder until
this is done. The E step can block the responder if the requester hasn’t had
time to extract the previous response.

This is the description. Now let's demonstrate this pattern in an example.
We’ll create a separate thread that receives a number and converts it to a
FizzBuzz word. We’ll also create the number generator thread that puts
requests, waits for results, and prints them. Let's start by defining the Pipe
type, the createPipe function, and the generic function sendRequest. Both
createPipe and sendRequest functions are operating by the MVar type, so
they should be impure. This means these functions are monadic in the IO
monad:

Listing 8.1 Simple request-response pattern

type Request a = MVar a
type Response b = MVar b
type Pipe a b   = (Request a, Response b)

createPipe :: IO (Pipe a b)
createPipe = do
  request <- newEmptyMVar
  response <- newEmptyMVar
  pure (request, response)

sendRequest :: Pipe a b -> a -> IO b
sendRequest (request, response) a = do
  putMVar request a
  takeMVar response

Next, we create a worker function that will listen to the channel forever and
process requests when they occur. It returns nothing useful to the client code.
worker :: Pipe a b -> (a -> b) -> IO ()
worker (request, response) f = forever $ do
  a <- takeMVar request
  putMVar response (f a)

TIP


I used forever from the Control.Monad module to make the do block
looping indefinitely. I could do that with an explicit recursion, but why not
save a line of code here. Nothing changes between the iterations, so this
function should fit just fine.
Then we parameterize this worker using our fizzBuzz function:
fizzBuzz x | isDivided x 15 = "FizzBuzz"
           | isDivided x 5 = "Buzz"
           | isDivided x 3 = "Fizz"
           | otherwise      = show x

isDivided x n = (x `mod` n) == 0

fizzBuzzProcessor :: Pipe Int String -> IO ()
fizzBuzzProcessor pipe = worker pipe fizzBuzz

Next, we create the generator function that will feed the remote worker
forever (now with the explicit recursion):
generator :: Pipe Int String -> Int -> IO ()
generator pipe i = do
  result <- sendRequest pipe i
  putStrLn ("[" ++ show i ++ "]: " ++ result)
  generator pipe (i + 1)

Almost done. The last step is to fork two threads and see what happens:
main = do
  pipe <- createPipe
  forkIO (fizzBuzzProcessor pipe)
  forkIO (generator pipe 0)
  threadDelay 10000000

As a result, we'll see an infinite request–response session with the FizzBuzz
words printed:
...
[112370]: Buzz
[112371]: Fizz
[112372]: 112372
[112373]: 112373
...

The connection is now established, and two threads play ping-pong
flawlessly. When organized carefully, this concurrent interaction won’t have
any issues, and it’s straightforward. Still, with this simple approach we aren’t
protected from constructing protocols that allow invalid communication.
What is Pipe a b after all? Those types a and b don’t seem to be dependent
anyhow, they only related by appearing in the Pipe type. Nothing stops us
from defining two ADTs for multiple requests and responses and hoping they
will be processed correctly. Let me define an abstract protocol for you and
demonstrate the problem.

Suppose, the requester wants some calculations to be done by the responder,
and also the result should be transferred back. Only two methods are
available: squaring an integer and reversing a string. The protocol may look
this:
data Request
  = Square Int
  | Reverse String

data Response
  = Squared Int
  | Reversed String

type CalculationPipe = Pipe Request Response

And now a requester risks getting a wrong response back. It wants to Square
10, but for some reason the response is Reversed "enola em evael" that is
definitely not what we would expect (Squared 100). Certainly, this is not
only the problem of the pattern we’re discussing here, it’s a common problem
specific to any communication protocols: generic HTTP and TCP protocols,
custom domain-specific protocols, data exchange between processes and so
on. What reaction a requester should have in front of invalid responses can
only be determined case-by-case accounting the requirements to that system
and some other additional considerations.

An interesting property of our inter-thread protocol through the MVar request
pattern is that we don’t have to risk a possible miscommunication somehow,
and we can make it 100% reliable and type safe by design. The pattern will
change a little, but only a little, and you’ll see how this variant of it
empowers the simulator.

8.2 Implementing the simulator
We need to wrap up some things we’ve learned so far. This is a huge toolbox
full of ideas, approaches, design patterns. However, if you get into the
Andromeda project that supports this book, you’ll realize there are not many
lines in it. We’ve tried to keep things easily expressible (domain-specific
languages and scripts), carefully separated (the SOLID principles and the
“low coupling, high cohesion” principle, pure and impure layers), well-
architectured (the Free monad architecture), testable (again, the Free monad
architecture), modular (all the code bits are nicely organized within the
project), concurrently safe (pure immutable immutable state or MVars) and
of course, simple (the KISS principle). We’ve achieved simplicity not
because we wanted to make things primitive at any costs, but because we
followed all those principles and ideas, and got simplicity as a result. This
wasn’t easy for sure because we had to learn many new tricks, but I’m sure
this was worth it.

So now we’d craft the last phrase in this long and rich story of implementing
of Andromeda Control Software. Figure 8.4 nicely outlines the work to be
done. We should implement the simulator itself and make it an actor. We also
should cast a network of devices which are also actors by the design. And we
should make them communicate, - better in a safe concurrent manner.

8.2.1 Simulator as an actor
Our simulator is an environment for simulation models to evolve and being
tested. Simultaneously, the simulator itself can be a specific actor that is
separated from the application and interfaced with the MVar request-response
pattern. It will be single-user for now, but introducing this protocol between
the application and the simulator may help in the future if we’d want to make
it multi-user. The simulator will be therefore placed into own process driven
by a dedicated server, and the MVar request-response protocol will be
converted into something like HTTP or gRPC.

Figure 8.6 illustrates the idea of a simulator as an actor:

Figure 8.6 Simulator as an actor
Now let’s develop a protocol for it. What exact methods should it support?
Three of them will be enough: run a simulation, start a simulation, and
shutdown the simulator. But wait… how is this redundancy in the protocol
not a mistake? Why run and start like it’s not synonyms?

In my vocabulary they are not synonyms indeed. If I need a simulation model
spinning indefinitely inside the simulator, I would start it, and I would not
expect any result back. But if my simulation is about to perform a finite
number of actions and finish soon, I would rather run it. So “start” implies
“no end” and “run” can be seen as a one-time action.

The request type below follows this consideration by having two methods
with different arguments. Pay your attention to the return values of
LogicControl arguments in RunSimulation and StartSimulation (listing
8.2):

Listing 8.2 Simulator request

data SimulatorRequest
  = forall a. RunSimulation (LogicControl a) (RunResult a)
        -- ^ Run the simulation, get the result back

  | StartSimulation (LogicControl ())
      -- ^ start spinning the simulation

  | ShutdownSimulator ShutdownResult
      -- ^ stop the simulation model and finish

Two result types occurring in the request are simple and yet interesting
because this is essentially the improvement of the MVar request-response
pattern:
newtype RunResult a    = RunResult (MVar a)
newtype ShutdownResult = ShutdownResult (MVar ())

What’s the idea? We’ve fused requests and responses into a single data
structure, and also tied a response MVar to its request method. Now, the only
possibility to properly process the RunSimulation method will be running the
simulation, getting some result of type a, and filling the MVar from the
RunResult value. As you can see, the MVar will also be of type a, that’s the
unavoidable condition provided by the design of the RunSimulation method.
The a type can be any but it should match between the script, its result and
the MVar. What about the ShutdownResult which declares an MVar of unit
(()), we don’t bother about getting results from the responder in this case.
Not the best design for sure because we might want to know about the issues
if termination went wrong. Consider improving this part of the protocol
yourself and see what options there are to indicate the requester about
problems.

The scheme of the improved MVar request-response pattern follows in figure
8.7:

Figure 8.7 Improved MVar request-response pattern
We shouldn’t count the case when the responder refuses filling the response
MVar because it’s a bug, not an acceptable behavior. Apart of that, there is
no possibility for the responder to send an invalid response.

TIP


We can also design protocols with sequences of requests, not just separate
ones. Try to figure it out how to build such a protocol as an exercise.

TIP


The SimulationRequest protocol can be easily updated if we want to run
multiple simulations within a single simulator. How would you design to
support this requirement?

Now, when we’ve elaborated the protocol, it’s time to implement the
simulator itself. There should be a separate thread for it, and a worker
function (simulatorWorker) that will act forever unless the corresponding
request is received. Also, the simulator actor should have its own state that
we call SimulatorRuntime. Let’s take a look at it, see listing 8.3:

Listing 8.3 Simulator implementation

data SimulatorRuntime = SimulatorRuntime
  { simRtControllerSimsVar                    #A
     :: MVar (Map Controller ControllerSim)   #A
  , simRtMessagesVar       :: MVar [String]   #B
  , simRtErrorsVar         :: MVar [String]   #C
  , simSimulationsVar      :: MVar [ThreadId] #D
  }

simulatorWorker :: SimulatorRuntime -> MVar SimulatorRequest -> IO ()
simulatorWorker runtime requestVar = do

  mbRequest <- tryReadMVar requestVar

  case mbRequest of
    Just (RunSimulation lc (RunResult resultVar)) -> do
      result <- runLogicControl runtime lc     #E
      putMVar resultVar result
      threadDelay 1000
      simulatorWorker runtime requestVar

     Just (StartSimulation lc) -> do            #F
       simThreadId <- forkIO $ runLogicControl runtime lc
       sims <- takeMVar $ simSimulationsVar runtime
       putMVar (simSimulationsVar runtime) (simThreadId : sims)
       threadDelay 1000
       simulatorWorker runtime requestVar

     Just (ShutdownSimulator (ShutdownResult resultVar)) -> do
       stopSimulation runtime                   #G
       putMVar resultVar ()

     Nothing -> do                                        #H
       threadDelay 1000
       simulatorWorker runtime requestVar
This code is simple, it just follows the scheme for the responder: get a
request, pattern-match it, evaluate the appropriate action, fill the response
variable, delay. Fall down with the recursion if needed or terminate. This is
our first actor, but actually we should prepare some data for it to be started.
Not a problem, let’s do that. The next function is like an animal to tame: you
feed it with SimulationRuntime, and it obediently returns you a leash in form
of SimulationControl:
data SimulatorControl = SimulatorControl
  { simulatorThreadId   :: ThreadId
  , simulatorRequestVar :: MVar SimulatorRequest
  }

startSimulator :: SimulatorRuntime -> IO SimulatorControl
startSimulator runtime = do
  simRequestVar <- newEmptyMVar
  simThreadId <- forkIO $ simulatorWorker runtime simRequestVar
  pure $ SimulatorControl simThreadId simRequestVar

We’ll see this idea of a control structure once again in the future because it’s
a convenient design pattern to have a representation of something that you
can interact with. In my tests for example I do this (notice that all the
functions have are self-explanatory):
it "Some test" $ do
  simRt <- createSimulatorRuntime

  simControl <- startSimulator simRt

  runSimulation simControl logicControlScript

  simControl <- stopSimulator simRt simControl

  -- do some checks of the SimulatorRuntime state

Where the runSimulation function just helps to make a proper request:
runSimulation :: SimulatorControl -> LogicControl a -> IO a
runSimulation control lc = do
  let SimulatorControl {simulatorRequestVar} = control
  resultVar <- newEmptyMVar
  let runResult = RunResult resultVar
  putMVar simulatorRequestVar $ RunSimulation lc runResult
  takeMVar resultVar
I definitely argue that this design of an actor-like simulator is super simple
and easy to grasp. With some help of the two design patterns and with a
strong adherence to a good low-coupled architecture we’ve achieved this nice
code.

What’s next? We should examine the interconnection between our domain-
specific scripts and the simulation model: how one becomes another, how do
we maintain state, what to do with concurrency and so on.

8.2.2 Stateful simulation model

The latest edition of the LogicControl eDSL had 4 methods:
data LogicControlMethod next
  = forall a. EvalHdl (Hdl a) (a -> next)
  | forall a. EvalDeviceControlMethod (DeviceControlMethod a) (a -> next)
  | Report Message (() -> next)
  | Store Key Value (() -> next)

Two first methods nest another eDSLs thus making a hierarchical structure
and empowering the LogicControl eDSL with more functionality. The
Report method was intended to communicate some message to the caller.
The idea of this method isn’t probably the best one because we can’t tell if
it’s about logging or another mechanism of broadcasting the message. And
what message it can be in particular, we can’t see from the definition. I would
say that this method is not designed well because it’s unobvious.

Let’s focus on the last one though. Store. It’s something about working with
a key-value storage. But where is the Load method? Our interface is clearly
incomplete. Let’s fix this (listing 8.4):

Listing 8.4 A primitive KV database interface

data LogicControlMethod next
  = Store Key Value (() -> next)
  | Load Key (Maybe Value -> next)

... other pieces of a Free monadic eDSL ...
-- | Stores a value with a key into Redis.
store :: Key -> Value -> LogicControl ()
-- | Load a value with a key into Redis.
load :: Key -> LogicControl (Maybe Value)

A sample script would look like this:
reupload :: LogicControl ()
reupload = do
  mbVal <- load "key"
  case mbVal of
    Just val -> store "another key" val
    Nothing -> pure ()     -- do nothing

We’re in our right to look at this as a mutable state that is accessible from the
business logic. This implies that our simulation model, that is a reflection of
the whole LogicControl ecosystem, should be stateful.

But how do we know what exactly state this is? Is it in-memory state? Or an
external key-value database? Or maybe not even a key-value one? And
what’s about thread-safety? The only source clarifying that we see is some
documentation for the methods (see the code above), but it can’t guarantee
that all the implementations will comply. The simulator is this violator to be
said. Instead of going to Redis, it will be placing all the values into the
internal in-memory dictionary. And you already know where to keep this
dictionary.
data SimulatorRuntime = SimulatorRuntime
  { ... other fields
  , simKeyValueDBVar :: MVar (Map Key (MVar Value))
  }

Notice the type: it’s an MVar of a map of MVar-ed values. It’s so special that
I’ll provide you with a picture (figure 8.8):

Figure 8.8 Data structure with two layers of concurrent mutability
Can you determine the characteristics of this data model design? What makes
it special is called granularity. The dashed lines here denote a separately
mutable, independent cluster of data. You don’t have to update the whole
structure if you only need to change the internal value. Even more, the
control over those values can be distributed across many threads with no
harm to concurrency. Compare with this:
type CoarseGrainedMap = MVar (Map Key Value)

With CoarseGrainedMap, you have to reupload new values into the map thus
making pure rearragements all the time. Given multiple updates of the same
key, you’ll be constructing this many new maps hence updating the MVar
many times. This can be less efficient than just lookuping an internal MVar
Value once and altering MVar without even the need to alter the outer MVar.

So we’ve just discussed a small part of a bigger problem called “coarse-
grained versus fine-granied” in correspondence to the data design. Can we
talk about it in depth? We should, actually, because it becomes sensitive
when dealing with STM-based concurrent models. And we’ll have this topic
covered in the next chapters.

What else needed for the rest of the simulation model? I categorize all the
devices as a part of it irrespective the fact that they are separate actors. The
reason is that they are being created during the LogicControl script
evaluation, namely due to the Hdl scripts. The Hdl actors will be organized
hierarchically. The topmost actor is a controller that has a responsibility to
interact with the outer world. There will be no way to bypass the controller in
order to access its internal devices (like sensors). Everything should go
through the interaction protocol the controller supports. I create a thread for
such a controller when interpreting over Hdl. Conveniently, we have the
SetupController method for this:

interpretHdlMethod :: SimulatorRuntime -> HdlMethod a -> IO a

interpretHdlMethod runtime (SetupController deviceName ctrlName passp next)
  -- fork a thread for the controller
  eCtrlSim <- makeControllerSim ctrlName passp
  ...      -- do the rest of initialization

I initialize the state for the actor in form of ControllerSim:
data ControllerSim = ControllerSim
  { ctrlSimThreadId :: ThreadId
    -- ^ controller worker thread
  , ctrlSimDef :: (ControllerName, ComponentPassport)
    -- ^ definition of the controller
  , ctrlSimDevicePartsVar :: MVar DevicePartSims
    -- ^ submissive actors representing devices
  , ctrlSimRequestVar :: MVar ControllerSimRequest
    -- ^ MVar request-response based protocol
  }

I create other actors during the interpretation of the Hdl script, namely, the
RegisterComponent method:

interpretHdlMethod :: SimulatorRuntime -> HdlMethod a -> IO a
interpretHdlMethod runtime (RegisterComponent ctrl idx passp next) = do
  ... -- a lot of code here, including threads forking and bookkeeping of ne

Finally, the Hdl script finishes interpreting, and we have the ready simulation
model accessible from the LogicControl scripts. For example you can ask
the needed measurements from certain domain-level devices:
reportBoostersTemperature :: LogicControl ()
reportBoostersTemperature = do
  (lBoosterCtrl, rBoosterCtrl) <- evalHdl createBoosters
  eTemperature <- readSensor lBoosterCtrl nozzle1t
  case eTemperature of
    Left err       -> report err
    Right (v1, v2) -> do
      report $ "T1 = " <> show v1 <> ", T2 = " <> show v2
      report "Temperature values are okay."

On the implementation level, this will be interpreted as an MVar request to
the specific actor through the whole hierarchy of actors. This is how the
whole simulator works, and this is how we utilized the Free monad pattern to
provide another implementation for the same high-level business-specific
language. Feel free to play with the Andromeda simulator application to get
